cross Scaling Laws of Ensemble Uncertainty in Language Models

Authors: Siamak Ravanbakhsh, David Nitchi, Khang Ngo

Abstract: Many use cases for large language models (LLMs)---from AI safety to exploration and human interaction---can benefit from epistemic uncertainty estimates. We argue that existing Bayesian Deep Learning solutions, which are often restricted to a fixed model family, fail to capture the full epistemic uncertainty at practical LLM scales. This is because the error due to the bounded capacity of the model family remains invisible to disagreement among models supported by the prior. We relate this error to the well-known power-law scaling of LLM loss with model size. We then show that a second power law quantifies the shrinking disagreement within ensembles as model size increases. Its slower decay relative to the capacity law above suggests that ensembles capture a growing share of epistemic uncertainty and become more competitive as an axis for scaling, compared to a single larger model. Finally, a third power law quantifies the loss of ensemble diversity as members share a larger fraction of training before branching, and therefore identifies a further limitation, beyond the capacity error, for LoRA ensembles and optimization-based or second-order methods that approximate uncertainty around a single training solution. At a high level, these findings suggest diverse ensembles can be a promising axis, both for scaling and uncertainty quantification at larger scales.

cross Sample-Efficient In-Context Adaptation via Sleep

Authors: Sangyun Lee, Zhang Enyan, Michael Xu, Hao Tang, Zinan Lin, Weijia Xu, Chris Quirk, Jianfeng Gao, Giulia Fanti, Xiaodong Liu

Abstract: The ability to learn and generalize from limited experience, or sample efficiency, is a cornerstone of intelligence. How can we build language models that turn a few user interactions, a few snippets of unfamiliar code, or a handful of experimental results provided in context into knowledge that generalizes? Prior work has found that sleep, a process where the model performs $N$ recurrent forward passes to refine its representations of a chunk of context, can improve long-context reasoning (specifically, within SSM-attention hybrid models). We study whether this method also improves in-context adaptation on general, non-synthetic tasks for transformer language models. To this end, we pre-train language models up to 1.2B-parameters on up to 100B tokens with sleep mechanisms. Notably, longer sleep (i.e., using a larger number of recurrent forward passes $N$) enables the model to learn faster in-context on multiple few-shot algorithmic, question-answering tasks, while also achieving lower next-token prediction loss on out-of-distribution evaluations. On held-out code, models with $N=2$ and $N=4$ match the no-sleep baseline's loss using $2.10\times$ and $2.39\times$ fewer tokens in-context, respectively. More broadly, we view our work as a step toward building models that have better tradeoffs between in-context adaptation and decoding latency.

cross MRBench: Benchmarking Physical Necessity Reasoning in Heterogeneous Multi-Robot Manipulation

Authors: Yanzhe Chen, Zhijun Cao, Ruihe An, Chang Gong, Mike Zheng Shou

Abstract: Recent advances in multi-robot manipulation have shown how robots can collaborate to accomplish complex tasks. However, successful coordination does not reveal which robots are actually needed. The same task may require different teams as robot capabilities change, resources become unavailable, or objects replace robotic support. We argue that reliable multi-robot manipulation requires physical necessity reasoning: understanding what a task demands, which resources can fulfill those demands, and which are truly necessary. We introduce MRBench, a benchmark for physical necessity reasoning in heterogeneous multi-robot manipulation. It comprises eight tasks with distinct concurrency and reach constraints, each evaluated under five configurations that change the required resources while preserving the task goal. Our evaluation reveals that existing coordination agents perform well with reference teams but struggle when robot embodiments change or passive objects can replace robotic support. To address this gap, we propose MRScissors, which separates task requirements from measured physical capabilities and identifies minimal feasible teams through constrained search. Across two model backbones, MRScissors achieves 85.4% and 91.2% team-selection accuracy, outperforming the strongest baselines by 10.4 and 9.5 points, respectively. Together, these findings expose a critical gap between coordinating robots and understanding their physical necessity.

cross Residual Guidance for Real-Time Interactive Video Generation

Authors: NaHyeon Park, Kyeong-Hwan Moon, Youjin Jeon, Hyunjung Shim

Abstract: Real-time interactive video generation allows users to change the prompt during generation to steer the evolving video. However, existing streaming models, which generate videos chunk by chunk, often keep following the previous prompt after a switch. We trace this failure to context inertia, where the previous chunk acts as a strong prior that suppresses the new prompt. Furthermore, we find that this inertia persists even when the cache retains only the previous chunk, yet discarding the context breaks visual continuity, which limits approaches that edit the cache. Rather than editing the cache, we propose Residual Guidance (RG), a simple, training-free method that counteracts context inertia with a correction measured outside the cache. Specifically, RG denoises the re-noised previous chunk once under the new prompt without the cache and adds the resulting residual to the early denoising steps of the next chunk, where its content is largely decided and the new prompt is most suppressed. Across settings ranging from a single switch to long multi-switch generation, RG substantially improves prompt switching capability on existing streaming models, including those with dedicated switching mechanisms, while largely retaining visual quality. As it requires no training or per-model tuning and adds only one forward pass per chunk, RG can be easily integrated into existing real-time video generation pipelines.

cross Reliable and grounded reporting of any computed tomography scan with FORA

Authors: Sezgin Er, Utku Turkbey, Alperen Gur, Omer Faruk Durugol, Suprosanna Shit, Murong Xu, Julia Wolleb, Jiayi Wang, Xiaoliang Wang, Hadrien Reynaud, Teodora Spasojevic, Dong Yang, Andriy Myronenko, Pengfei Guo, Yufan He, Can Zhao, Marc Edgar, Andreas Maier, Michael Moor, Mehmet Kemal Ozdemir, Daguang Xu, Bernhard Kainz, Bjoern Menze, Ibrahim Ethem Hamamci

Abstract: Artificial intelligence systems for computed tomography (CT) remain largely restricted to specific anatomical regions, examination types or predefined abnormalities, limiting their ability to provide comprehensive and spatially grounded interpretations. Its architecture combines multiple-instance learning with an interpretable concept bottleneck that connects spatial evidence, radiological findings and report generation. We first developed and benchmarked the architecture on chest radiographs, achieving state-of-the-art performance, before training a separate model on three-dimensional CT examinations. These results show that a single model can combine broad abnormality recognition, spatial localization and report generation across 12 CT examination types, using routine reports without lesion-level supervision.

cross Field Life: Particle Life as Interacting Chemical Fields

Authors: Iliya Zhechev

Abstract: Field Life is a cellular automaton inspired by the interaction dynamics of Particle Life. In Particle Life, each particle has a colour, and an interaction matrix sets how strongly each colour attracts or repels each other colour. In Field Life, each colour is a channel of the automaton, and each cell holds a concentration of every colour. One step of the rule has three parts. Each colour is convolved with a ring-shaped kernel, which measures how much of that colour surrounds a cell. The interaction matrix combines these measurements into an attraction for each colour. The mass of each colour then moves toward higher attraction, in a way that conserves mass. A random matrix and a random initial state are usually enough to produce moving structure: bodies that glide, bodies that shed diffuse clouds, and gliders that break up into smaller gliders. We sample rules at random, keep the rules that move and form structure, and describe the patterns that appear: colours that sort into regions, colours that form stripes, and gliders, compact bodies that travel across the grid. The simulation and 120 saved rules, which we call worlds, are released.

cross Path-probability gradients for optimizing force fields against dynamical observables

Authors: Zhongyao Wang, Lun Song, Yakun Zeng, Xingao Gong, Rui Shi, Mao Su

Abstract: Machine learning force fields inherit the errors of the electronic-structure methods used to generate their training data, and refining them against experimental measurements provides a direct route beyond this limit. However, such refinement remains difficult for dynamical observables because differentiating chaotic molecular trajectories yields unstable gradients. Here, we introduce PathGrad, which differentiates Langevin path probabilities instead of the trajectories themselves. The resulting gradient estimator is accumulated on the fly from thermal noise and force derivatives, and its variance remains stable as the trajectory length increases. We validated the gradient estimator against finite-difference calculations and used it to fine-tune molten-copper and water potentials against experimental diffusion coefficients together with structural constraints. For water, jointly optimizing diffusion and the oxygen-oxygen radial distribution function also improves the predictions of shear viscosity and rotational relaxation, neither of which is included in the optimization objective. Alignment at a single temperature also improves the apparent activation energies of multiple dynamical observables, bringing their temperature dependences closer to experiments. Moreover, the optimized potential exhibits smaller force errors relative to an independent, higher-level quantum-chemical reference, indicating that alignment with experiments improves the fidelity of the potential energy surface beyond the fitted targets. PathGrad thus establishes a general framework for incorporating experimental observables directly into force field optimizations.

cross MXFP4 for LLM Inference: Complementary Post-Training Quantization Techniques and Their Interactions

Authors: Natalia Frumkin, Alireza Khodamoradi, Kristof Denolf, Eric Dellinger, Stuart Biles

Abstract: Recent advances in low-precision representation have introduced several microscaling numerical formats for LLM quantization. By sharing a scale factor across groups of 16 or 32 elements, these formats enable aggressive low-precision quantization with minimal accuracy degradation, particularly when combined with modern PTQ algorithms. Their accuracy depends on how the numerical format interacts with rotation, PTQ, and scale selection. We study the composability of PTQ configurations across six LLMs and three microscaling formats: MXFP6, MXFP4, and NVFP4, systematically varying Hadamard rotation granularity, PTQ algorithm, and scale-selection strategy. Our central finding is that effective quantization recipes are format dependent: both MXFP4 and NVFP4 support accurate 4-bit inference, but techniques that benefit one do not necessarily transfer to the other. MXFP4 benefits substantially from rotation and error-correcting PTQ, whereas NVFP4 gains less from rotation, consistent with its finer-grained scales capturing more within-block variation. Building on these findings, we introduce Midmax, a calibration-free scale-selection strategy that mitigates clipping error in power-of-two block scales and improves accuracy across a majority of the evaluated MXFP4 configurations. We show that pairing MXFP6 activations with MXFP4 weights recovers most of the accuracy lost at 4-bit and, on Llama-3.1-8B, matches or exceeds our in-house BF16 baseline. An additional study finds that augmenting MXFP4 with a per-tensor macroscale, a mechanism intrinsic to NVFP4, does not improve average accuracy, suggesting that global rescaling alone does not address the limitations of E8M0 block scales for MXFP4 quantization. Together, these results provide practical guidance for combining numerical formats, rotation, PTQ, and scale selection for accurate low-precision LLM inference.

cross Adaptive Electromagnetic Transient-level Digital Twins for Data Centers via In-Context Learning with LLMs

Authors: Amit Jena, Bin Wang, Aoxia Chen, Brad Chadwell, Billy Yancey, Le Xie

Abstract: The rapid growth of artificial intelligence (AI) workloads has significantly increased data center (DC) power demand. Frequent operational and configurational changes, such as load surges caused by dynamic workload allocation, can induce electromagnetic transients (EMTs) whose accurate prediction is critical for reliable DC operation and maintenance. Although high-fidelity EMT solvers are widely adopted for transient analysis, their computational cost limits large-scale and multi-scenario studies. Digital twins address this computational bottleneck by emulating the behavior of a physical system and enabling fast prediction in place of expensive high-fidelity simulation. However,data-driven digital twins are non-adaptive and fail to generalize once the DC configuration changes. To address this limitation, this paper proposes an adaptive EMT-level digital twin for DCs based on in-context learning (ICL) with large language models (LLMs). We formulate the EMT prediction task as a system identification problem under parametric uncertainty and develop a GPT-2-based framework that adapts to unseen DC configurations at inference time from a small set of EMT trajectory samples, without model retraining. The proposed framework is validated on three representative EMT scenarios and benchmarked against three baseline non-adaptive twins. Results demonstrate superior prediction accuracy, improved adaptability to unseen operating conditions, and substantial computational speedups compared to high-fidelity EMT simulation.

Abstract: Online hate speech is an ever-growing issue with negative impacts on both groups and individuals, potentially causing users to withdraw from online conversations and debate. cross Motion Conditioned World Modeling for Embodied Visual Tracking

Authors: Jiahao Zhang, Xuan Yao, Junyu Gao, Changsheng Xu

Abstract: World models support embodied decision-making by predicting future environment states. In embodied visual tracking, uncertain target motion and robot-induced viewpoint changes jointly affect visual observations, making future-state prediction challenging. We propose Motion-Conditioned World Modeling, which incorporates predicted target-motion distributions and robot ego-motion into latent state transitions. A Geometry--Progress module jointly models geometric paths and single-step travel distances through shared motion modes and path-conditioned progress distributions. This formulation captures uncertainty in route selection and motion progress while preserving their dependence. Action-induced coordinate transformations express predicted paths in the next robot frame, providing geometrically grounded conditions for future visual-state prediction. cross Efficient Video Retrieval Augmented Generation via Token Allocation

Authors: Ayush Barik, Fan Lai, Francis Y. Yan

Abstract: Efficient long-video understanding requires identifying relevant evidence while minimizing the tokens processed by vision-language models (VLMs). Existing video retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) methods rely on frame selection and token compression, but overlook how much each additional token is worth. We introduce Lembas, a plug-and-play framework that formulates video RAG as a token allocation problem over frames and subtitles under a shared budget. Our key insight is that the value of a token diminishes within each item and decreases further as similar evidence receives more tokens. Lembas captures both effects in a single objective and solves it with an efficient iterative water-filling algorithm that has a provable convergence guarantee. For multi-turn conversations, Lembas reuses the tokens processed in earlier turns and refines relevance with the VLM's attention at no extra cost. Our evaluations show that Lembas improves accuracy by up to 7.2 percentage points over state-of-the-art baselines at comparable compute. On multi-turn video understanding, Lembas achieves up to 1.5 percentage points higher accuracy with 1.7-17.3x fewer TFLOPs.

cross Do Vision-Language Models Know Their Own Visual Weaknesses? Probing Capability-Level Metacognition

Authors: Dohoon Kim, Taesup Moon

Abstract: Metacognition includes knowing one's own strengths and weaknesses across kinds of tasks, not only judging whether a single answer is correct. We test whether vision-language models (VLMs) know in advance which kinds of visual tasks and image conditions are hard for them, across a wide range of open and proprietary models. Because some tasks are hard for every model, we separate what a model knows about this shared difficulty from what it knows about itself. We find that VLMs know little in advance about their visual weaknesses. They misjudge their overall accuracy and do not even know which kinds of tasks are hard for every model, and most misjudge how degraded images affect them. We further train VLMs to predict their own accuracy, which teaches them their own level of accuracy on kinds unseen in training but not which kinds are hard for them. However, information about these weaknesses appears once a model starts solving. On a few unlabeled questions of a kind, the probability a model assigns to its own answers follows weaknesses particular to the model, across kinds of tasks and image conditions. VLMs thus hold information about their own weaknesses that their stated predictions do not yet reflect. By separating what is particular to a model from what holds for every model, our evaluation offers a test for methods that aim to teach VLMs their own weaknesses, and the answer probability offers them a signal to start from.

cross "I Do Intend to Deliver": Will LLMs Run a Marketplace Advance-Fee Scam?

Authors: Megan Squire

Abstract: Online fraud is traditionally limited by the cost of the human labor needed to talk to potential victims. We ask whether hosted large language models will now supply that labor. We prompted ten off-the-shelf models to play the seller in the "free item, just pay delivery" advance-fee scam common on buy-and-sell sites, using a plain system prompt with no jailbreak. Each model played 30 conversations against each of four buyer personas, from credulous to guarded, with the same model playing the buyer (1,200 conversations). We label each conversation by what happened to the buyer's money, and validate the labels against two human coders. 737 conversations (61%) ended with the buyer paying in advance. Eight of the ten models played in almost every conversation, and running both sides cost about 2.5 cents per payment. Only the guarded buyer, who wanted a findable identity, resisted, and the most willing sellers got past it by inventing names and phone numbers. Sellers that recognized the scam still took the money in 133 conversations, usually reasoning about their own honesty rather than the buyer's risk. Several models protected the buyer without refusing, by conceding payment on delivery, so refusal rates alone misstate safety. We release the benchmark, prompts, code and labels.

cross Attestation is All You Need: Toward a Zero-Trust Architecture for HPC AI Agents

Abstract: AI agents are beginning to automate data analysis, orchestration, and simulation steering in HPC environments, but they introduce a security problem that existing cluster defenses do not address: the hijacked authorized agent. cross CAPER: PartGround and PlanRefine for Reliable Manual-Guided Robotic Assembly Planning

Authors: Hanzhao Xi, Zhonghao Li, Suxuan Liu, Shanghan Zhou, Zesheng Chen, Qiufeng Wang, Yaran Chen

Abstract: Vision-Language-Models (VLMs) used in robot task planning to derive assembly procedures from existing manuals may correctly interpret individual assembly actions yet confuse exact part identities, predict unsupported Part IDs, or produce inconsistent parts across assembly steps. cross On Potentiality and Actuality Of Foundation Models

Authors: Fabian Gr\"oger, Shuo Wen, Maria Brbi\'c

Abstract: Foundation models are built through large-scale pretraining, often followed by post-training, and are then adapted to specific tasks and behaviors. But, does the adaptation add truly new capabilities, or the base model already holds it? We decompose the update induced by different adaptations into a component shared across all inputs and a residual that varies with the input. We find that the shared component suffices for most of the adaptation gain and can be learned directly: a shift adapter that learns a constant vector per layer, identical for every input, reproduces the behavior of LoRA and full fine-tuning. We call this the potentiality principle of foundation models: the base model already contains the capacity needed for task-specific expertise. To adapt to a new task, the model just needs to be actualized, i.e., pushed in a certain direction via a constant, input-independent shift. We show the implications of this principle on parameter-efficient adaptation, steering, and convergence across models, and how it explains the neural thickets phenomenon.

cross One, No One and One Hundred Thousand CTs: Testing Anatomical Control in Text-to-CT Generation

Authors: Francesca Pia Panaccione, Eugenio Lomurno, Matteo Matteucci

Abstract: Recent advances in deep learning and the public availability of computed tomography (CT) datasets with paired reports have enabled many Text-to-CT generators. However, their evaluations almost always differ in preprocessing and metrics, which hinders comparison. Moreover, their protocols almost never test how editing the report changes the CT, not even at the same seed, so the report is often assumed to control the anatomy without evidence of how much and how consistently it does. We therefore propose CtrlCT, a toolkit that tests two properties of a generator, Credibility and Control. Credibility tests compare generated CTs, as a population, with real CTs and reports; Control tests are metamorphic and need no reference CT: at a fixed seed, editing one finding should change it as requested, keep negated findings absent and shift distant anatomy no more than a new seed, and the change should hold across seeds. A Report Card summarises all measures and insights for each generator. We benchmark five public generators on 940 chest CTs and 687 edits of pleural effusion and cardiomegaly, holding out a sixth to test the toolkit. The analyses proposed in the literature do not reveal this. We release CtrlCT, its edits, seeds and 30,525 synthetic CTs after acceptance.

Authors: Hanzhen Wang, Jiaming Xu, Guohao Dai

Abstract: Speculative decoding accelerates large language model (LLM) generation using a lightweight draft. Existing methods evaluate complete recipes under different training configurations, leaving component effects, interactions, and composition benefits unclear. cross Embodied AI Fails the Mirror Test: How a Simple Mirror Breaks Robot Policies

Abstract: Can a simple mirror expose the limitations of today's robotic manipulation policies? Real-robot experiments with $\pi_{0.5}$ and FastWAM also show lower overall success rates under the upright-mirror and mirrored-tabletop conditions. These findings reveal a shared vulnerability to reflective environments across diverse robot policies and motivate reflection-aware evaluation of embodied intelligence. cross Can a Frontier LLM Repair a Quantized Network by Tool-Free Weight-Space Programming?

Authors: Steffen Urban

Abstract: We give four large language models (LLMs) of the Claude family the complete weights of a 20k-parameter CIFAR-10 CNN as text and ask them to produce a 4-bit network without tools, data or the ability to run inference. Standard round-to-nearest (RTN) quantization to 4 bit lowers the test accuracy from 81.4% to 60.4%. We design four evaluation protocols that differ in the input and task description given to the LLMs and analyze their responses. In the first two protocols the LLMs are given the float and the 4-bit RTN network and are told that the accuracy has dropped significantly. In the last two they receive only the float network and the task to quantize it, which tests whether they apply an optimization on their own or fall back to plain RTN. This is 3.5 pp below the float network and 2.8 pp below AdaRound, which learns the rounding from calibration data. Given only the float network, the LLMs name error-cancelling corrections but do not apply them and fall back to plain RTN, at most with mildly clipped scales, which still lifts Fable 5.2's quantized network to 70.0%. cross ClusterVAD: Threshold-Free Unsupervised Voice Activity Detection with Pre-Trained Neural Representations

Authors: Karl El Hajal, Mathew Magimai. -Doss

Abstract: Voice activity detection (VAD) is one of the first steps in speech processing pipelines, from transcription and diarization to speech language models. Supervised detectors need labelled data and inherit the domain and annotation conventions of their training corpora. Existing unsupervised detectors rely on hand-crafted acoustic features which cannot benefit from data and degrade in far-field, noisy or atypical speech. We propose ClusterVAD, an unsupervised VAD built on frozen speech foundation model representations. Fitted once on a small set of untranscribed speech, it clusters the representations and labels each cluster as speech or non-speech with sonority, an acoustic measure. At inference, a frame-level speech probability is computed from the cluster assignments, and a two-state hidden Markov model fitted to each recording enables threshold-free detection. Across benchmarks covering meetings, far-field and noisy recordings, Mandarin and dysarthric speech, ClusterVAD obtains the lowest detection error on pause-level references and is competitive on speaker turn-level references. These results indicate that speech foundation models inherently capture the distinction between speech and non-speech, which can be leveraged without training a detector.

cross On the Creation of Object-Centric Event Logs from Patient Journeys

Authors: Saimir Bala, Neven Borak, Maximilian K\"onig, Anjo Seidel, Zeinab Soleimani, Lorenzo Famiglini, Lev Petrov, Jonas Witt, Mathias Weske

Abstract: Interactions between patients and health systems as well as conditions that individuals experience during a disease are represented in patient journeys. Therefore, patient journeys are important assets to investigate how care is being provided, and how health systems can be designed to better suit the needs of patients. While there is initial work on patient journeys from a process mining perspective with traditional case notions, object-centric process mining techniques have yet to be covered. This is unfortunate, since due to the rich structure of objects and their relationships, object-centric process mining approaches are particularly promising in this context. This paper addresses this gap by investigating object-centric event log (OCEL) generation from patient journeys. Based on a list of challenges and requirements, the paper presents an approach for the creation of OCELs from patient journey data. The approach is evaluated on a synthetic patient cohort provided by our industry partner.

cross AIChipBench: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Automated AI Accelerator Design

Authors: Guangyuan Wang, Chengran Li, Minchen Luo, Jichen Feng, Mengdi Wang, Yifan Zhang

Abstract: A language-model decoder carries state between decoding steps, and in hardware a wrong state update can leave the current token correct and corrupt the output several tokens later, so a design can pass output tests and still be wrong. AIChipBench asks what LLM coding agents need to build such hardware correctly. Agents implement quantized Qwen3.5, Gemma 3, Gemma 4, and Llama 3.2 decoders in synthesizable register-transfer-level (RTL) code against an executable integer reference that specifies the outputs and the stored state at every decoding step. They debug against public tests under fixed budgets; their frozen designs are graded on held-out token sequences, resets, memory stalls, and synthesis, and a second track asks them to speed up a correct design at nearly fixed area. Across 9,136 attempts by four coding agents, the difficulty lies in stored state. Feedback that points to the first state mismatch raises acceptance over output-only feedback in all 12 agent--task cells of a matched study, by 25.0 to 46.9 percentage points, and halves the agent-hours per accepted design. Regraded on new sequences and conditions, 45 of 69 accepted designs fail within the 32 steps their tests required. More than a third of attempts to speed up a correct design end without one. An overall score ranks Sol 5.6 and Fable 5.1 above Astra 6 and Opus 5.5, and both halves of the instances give this order in all 200 random splits. Hardware agents therefore need debugging feedback that exposes stored state, and their benchmarks need final tests well beyond the development cases.

cross EventDiffusion: Learning Native Event Stream Distribution from Videos

Authors: Karthik Sundar Senthilkumar, Christopher Thirgood, Simon Hadfield

Abstract: Event cameras capture asynchronous brightness changes at high temporal resolution, but scarce recordings constrain data-driven research. Sparse RGB observations cannot uniquely determine intervening events since identical frames may correspond to streams with different activity, polarities, and timing. Existing simulators largely rely on prescribed sensor dynamics and estimated brightness trajectories, whose assumptions limit the diversity and fidelity of plausible event streams. We introduce EventDiffusion, a hierarchical generative framework that directly learns the conditional distribution of native asynchronous event streams from real recordings. EventDiffusion decomposes generation into three probabilistic processes: Event Activity Diffusion (EAD) learns stochastic discrete event counts; Event Spatial Allocation (ESA) distributes these counts to individual pixels while preserving cardinality; and Event Timing Diffusion (ETD) learns continuous asynchronous timestamp distributions. We also introduce CEFS, an event-stream realism metric combining event activity and polarity with multiscale space-time structure and temporal fidelity. Across all datasets, EventDiffusion achieves double the accuracy of SOTA methods and demonstrates robustness across different cameras at any resolution.

cross Draft Calibration for Speculative Decoding: Accelerating LLM Serving by Matching Draft and Target Distributions

Authors: Yuxing Liu, Wei Zuo, Walid Krichene, Tong Zhang, Li Zhang

Abstract: Speculative decoding accelerates large language model (LLM) inference by employing a lightweight draft model to propose draft tokens and allowing the target model to verify the new tokens in parallel to improve inference-time compute utilization. The speedup relies on the acceptance probability of draft tokens in target verification, which is directly related to the gap $\|p-q\|_1$ between the target distribution $p$ and the draft distribution $q$. In this paper, we study draft calibration of speculative decoding to improve the speedup of speculative decoding, where we employ post-hoc methods to calibrate the draft distribution to mitigate the distribution gap. We observe that though current community open-source draft models may learn the top candidate token correctly, they are generally underconfident compared to the target model, suggesting a consistent distribution mismatch. To mitigate the effect of this mismatch on the acceptance rate of the draft models, we try various post-hoc calibration methods, including standard temperature scaling and token-wise calibration. Our results suggest that a standard temperature scaling applied to the draft distribution improves the acceptance length by up to approximately 10\% with almost no additional cost across different tasks. Throughput analysis further verifies that this improvement in serving efficiency. Analysis of more general calibration methods reveals further potential room for improvement.

cross OmniAct: One Policy, One Million Embodiments

Authors: Xiang Fan, Yuheng Wang, Jiayuan Yan, Bohan Fang, Wisdom Oluchi Ikezogwo, Ainaz Eftekhar, Max Argus, Jason Ren, Jiafei Duan, Ranjay Krishna

Abstract: A single policy that controls diverse robots must not only transfer task knowledge across embodiments, but also adapt its behavior to each robot's capabilities. Existing approaches facilitate transfer through shared action representations, but these representations do not necessarily capture how differences in robot morphology should shape manipulation strategies. We introduce OmniAct, a cross-embodiment manipulation policy that conditions directly on each robot's kinematics in a specific scene to produce embodiment-specific behavior. This kinematic graph represents the robot's joint positions, motion axes, and connectivity to its visual observation, allowing the policy to account for how its embodiment related to objects and surfaces in the scene. A flow-matching policy predicts relative joint motions for each node, enabling control across robots with different degrees of freedom and kinematic configurations. To learn how physical structure shapes behavior at scale, we procedurally design and filter one million physically feasible robot embodiments; next, we develop algorithms to generate robot trajectories in simulation for all embodiments; finally, we train across diverse simulated manipulation tasks. On 2,000 embodiment-scene-held-out evaluation episodes, OmniAct achieves 63.0% success, compared to 17.8% for pi 0.5 and 57.9% for the strongest baseline; on the task families GPT-6 Astra was evaluated on, it achieves 64.7% against GPT-6 Astra's 41.1%. On a previously unseen real-world robot, it achieves nearly twice the success rate of the next best method, MolmoAct2. Beyond task success, we observe that OmniAct learns distinct manipulation strategies that exploit the capabilities and accommodate the limitations of different morphologies. Project: https://omniact.github.io/

URLs: https://omniact.github.io/

replace Fortune: Formula-Driven Reinforcement Learning for LLM Reasoning over Complex Tables

Authors: Lang Cao, Jingxian Xu, Hanbing Liu, Jinyu Wang, Mengyu Zhou, Haoyu Dong, Shi Han, Dongmei Zhang

Abstract: Tables are a fundamental medium for organizing and analyzing data, making table reasoning an essential capability for intelligent systems. Although large language models (LLMs) exhibit strong general reasoning abilities, they still struggle with accurate numerical reasoning over tabular data, particularly in complex table settings that require more than simple relational lookup. Spreadsheet formulas provide a powerful and expressive interface for executable symbolic operations, enabling sophisticated reasoning patterns that remain largely underexplored by existing LLMs. In this paper, we introduce Formula Tuning (Fortune), a formula-driven reinforcement learning (RL) framework that trains LLMs to generate executable spreadsheet formulas for question answering over tabular data, using execution success and answer correctness as reward signals. Building upon Fortune, we train Formula-R1 and evaluate its performance across seven table reasoning benchmarks, demonstrating competitive performance, particularly on tasks involving complex tables and multi-step numerical computation. Furthermore, Formula-R1-Plus, which integrates textual and formula-based reasoning, achieves further performance improvements. Beyond these empirical results, we provide insights into the roles of reinforcement learning and executable formula representations in table reasoning, highlighting the broader potential of symbolic RL to enhance the reasoning capabilities of LLMs.

replace Local Policy Manifolds for Efficient Multi-Objective Reinforcement Learning

Authors: Qiyue Xia, Tianwei Wang, J. Michael Herrmann

Abstract: Multi-objective reinforcement learning (MORL) aims at optimising several, often conflicting goals to improve the flexibility and reliability of RL in practical tasks. This is typically achieved by finding a set of diverse, non-dominated policies that form a Pareto front in the performance space. However, constructing such a policy set remains computationally demanding, as it requires finding not a single optimal policy, but a set of policies to cover a wide range of trade-offs among the objectives. We introduce LLE-MORL, an approach that traces policy manifolds by utilising the local relationship between the high-dimensional policy parameter space and the performance space. This structured representation enables an efficient search within contiguous solution domains via locally linear extrapolation, allowing for the rapid generation of high-quality solutions without extensive retraining. We also provide a theoretical analysis of the policy manifold structure in order to specify the applicability of the method. Experiments across diverse continuous control domains demonstrate that LLE-MORL consistently achieves higher Pareto front quality and efficiency than state-of-the-art approaches.

replace CrafterDojo: An Integrated Research Suite for Building Open-Ended Embodied Agents in Crafter

Authors: Junyeong Park, Hyeonseo Cho, Sungjin Ahn

Abstract: Foundation models are central to building general-purpose embodied agents. Minecraft has become the dominant testbed for this paradigm, but its computational and engineering overhead limits rapid iteration and controlled analysis. Crafter offers a lightweight alternative yet lacks the foundation model ecosystem required for such research. We introduce CrafterDojo, a suite of foundation models, datasets, and data-generation toolkits that establishes Crafter as a testbed for foundation-model-based embodied intelligence, providing CrafterVPT, CrafterCLIP, and CrafterSteve-1. To address Crafter's data scarcity, CrafterDojo includes automatic tools that generate diverse synthetic demonstrations and aligned video-language pairs, yielding the CrafterPlay and CrafterCaption datasets. Experiments show that these datasets support effective foundation model training and enable hierarchical agents that outperform non-hierarchical baselines on long-horizon tasks. We release all code, models, and datasets at https://github.com/frechele/CrafterDojo.

replace Patient-Zero: Scaling Synthetic Patient Agents to Real-World Distributions without Real Patient Data

Authors: Yunghwei Lai, Ziyue Wang, Weizhi Ma, Yang Liu

Abstract: Synthetic data generation with Large Language Models (LLMs) has emerged as a promising solution in the medical domain to mitigate data scarcity and privacy constraints. However, existing approaches remain constrained by their derivative nature, relying on real-world records, which pose privacy risks and distribution biases. Furthermore, current patient agents face the Stability-Plasticity Dilemma, struggling to maintain clinical consistency during dynamic inquiries. To address these challenges, we introduce Patient-Zero, a novel framework for ab initio patient simulation that requires no real medical records. Our Medically-Aligned Hierarchical Synthesis framework generates comprehensive and diverse patient records from medical knowledge via stratified attribute permutation. replace Efficient Prediction of pass@k Scaling in Large Language Models

Authors: Joshua Kazdan, Colin Sullivan, Rylan Schaeffer, Youssef Allouah, Kyssen Yu, Noam Levi, Sanmi Koyejo

Abstract: Assessing the capabilities and risks of frontier AI systems is a critical area of research; recent work has shown that repeated sampling can dramatically increase both. For instance, repeated sampling allows models to solve difficult math and coding problems, but it may also increase their risk of being jailbroken. Such results raise a crucial question: how can one accurately predict a model's behavior when scaled to a massive number of attempts, given a vastly smaller sampling budget? This question is directly relevant to model providers, who serve hundreds of millions of users daily, and to governmental regulators, who seek to prevent harms. To answer this question, we make three contributions. First, we find that standard methods for fitting these laws suffer from statistical shortcomings that hinder predictions, especially in data-limited scenarios. Second, we remedy these shortcomings by introducing a robust estimation framework, which uses a beta-binomial distribution to generate more accurate predictions from limited data. Third, we propose a dynamic sampling strategy that allocates a greater budget to harder problems. Combined, these innovations enable more reliable prediction of rare risks and capabilities at a fraction of the computational cost.

replace Repairing Reward Functions with Feedback to Mitigate Reward Hacking

Authors: Stephane Hatgis-Kessell, Logan Mondal Bhamidipaty, Emma Brunskill

Abstract: Human-designed reward functions for reinforcement learning (RL) agents are frequently misaligned with the humans' true, unobservable objectives, and thus act only as proxies. Optimizing for a misspecified proxy reward function often induces reward hacking, resulting in a policy misaligned with the human's true objectives. An alternative is to perform RL from human feedback, which involves learning a reward function from scratch by collecting human preferences over pairs of trajectories. However, building such datasets is costly. To address the limitations of both approaches, we propose Preference-Based Reward Repair (PBRR): an automated iterative framework that repairs a human-specified proxy reward function by learning an additive, transition-dependent correction term from preferences. A manually specified reward function can yield policies that are highly suboptimal under the ground-truth objective, yet corrections on only a few transitions may suffice to recover optimal performance. To identify and correct for those transitions, PBRR uses a targeted exploration strategy and a new preference-learning objective. We prove in tabular domains PBRR has a cumulative regret that matches, up to constants, that of prior preference-based RL methods. In addition, on a suite of reward-hacking benchmarks, PBRR consistently outperforms baselines that learn a reward function from scratch from preferences or modify the proxy reward function using other approaches, requiring substantially fewer preferences to learn high performing policies.

replace MADRA: Multi-Agent Debate for Risk-Aware Embodied Planning

Authors: Junjian Wang, Lidan Zhao, Xi Sheryl Zhang

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) exhibit impressive reasoning capabilities but often suffer from "Embodied Semantic Hallucinations" - generating plans that are semantically fluent but physically unsafe due to a lack of grounded common sense. Existing safety alignment methods, such as RLHF or naive safety prompting, typically fall into a "Safety-Utility Trade-off," resulting in severe over-rejection of benign household instructions. To address this, we propose MADRA (Multi-Agent Debate for Risk Awareness), a training-free cognitive architecture that mimics System-2 deliberation. MADRA introduces a meta-cognitive Critical Agent that evaluates peer debates using a structured argumentation framework derived from the Toulmin Model, effectively mitigating the "herd mentality" in multi-agent systems. We also introduce SafeAware-VH, a benchmark featuring adversarial safe instructions designed to probe agents' sensitivity to physical risks. Extensive experiments demonstrate that MADRA breaks the Pareto frontier, achieving over 90% rejection of unsafe tasks while maintaining high utility, significantly outperforming standard Chain-of-Thought and single-agent reflection baselines.

replace MemVerse: Multimodal Memory for Lifelong Learning Agents

Authors: Junming Liu, Yifei Sun, Weihua Cheng, Haodong Lei, Yirong Chen, Licheng Wen, Xuemeng Yang, Daocheng Fu, Pinlong Cai, Nianchen Deng, Yi Yu, Botian Shi, Ding Wang

Abstract: Despite the remarkable advancements in multimodal large language models across various domains, AI agents still lack the ability to retain past information. Without reliable memory, agents struggle to leverage historical context, impairing long-horizon reasoning and coherent interactions in dynamic environments. In this paper, we introduce MemVerse, a model-agnostic, plug-and-play memory framework that combines fast parametric recall with hierarchical retrieval, enabling scalable and adaptive multimodal intelligence. MemVerse maintains short-term memory for recent context while transforming raw experiences into structured long-term memory graphs, empowering continuous learning and autonomous knowledge evolution. To meet real-time demands, we introduce a periodic distillation mechanism that compresses essential knowledge from the cognitive graph into the parametric model, providing fast and differentiable recall while preserving interpretability. Extensive experiments show that MemVerse boosts multimodal reasoning with superior efficiency, allowing agents to maintain coherence and adapt seamlessly throughout extended interactions. Our code is available at https://github.com/KnowledgeXLab/MemVerse.

replace BEAVER: An Efficient Deterministic LLM Verifier

Authors: Nalin Wadhwa, Tarun Suresh, Debangshu Banerjee, Gagandeep Singh

Abstract: As large language models (LLMs) transition from research prototypes to production systems, practitioners need reliable ways to verify model outputs before they are deployed. While sampling-based estimates provide an ad-hoc intuition of model behavior, they offer no sound guarantees. We present BEAVER, the first practical framework for computing deterministic, sound bounds on the probability that an LLM's response to a prompt satisfies a property. Given a prompt and an oracle, BEAVER systematically explores the model's output distribution using a *token trie* and a *frontier*, maintaining provably sound bounds at every iteration. We formalize the LLM verification problem, prove that BEAVER's bounds are sound and that its selection strategy Max-$\mu$ is optimal for verification upto any tolerance. On 3 important safety tasks across 5 popular LLMs, BEAVER certifies up to $7.6\times$ more prompts as risky than a sampling-based verifier while using fewer forward passes, and its per-prompt bounds extend to statistical certificates over distributions of prompts.

replace The Dead Salmons of AI Interpretability

Authors: Maxime M\'eloux, Giada Dirupo, Fran\c{c}ois Portet, Maxime Peyrard

Abstract: In a striking neuroscience study, the authors placed a dead salmon in an MRI scanner and showed it images of humans in social situations. Astonishingly, standard analyses of the time reported brain regions predictive of social emotions. The explanation, of course, was not supernatural cognition but a cautionary tale about misapplied statistical inference. In AI interpretability, reports of similar "dead salmon" artifacts abound: feature attribution, probing, sparse auto-encoding, and even causal analyses can produce plausible-looking explanations for randomly initialized neural networks. Upon inspection of this phenomenon our position is to argue for a pragmatic statistical-causal reframing: explanations of computational systems should be treated as parameters of a (statistical) model, inferred from computational traces. This perspective goes beyond simply measuring statistical variability of explanations due to finite sampling of input data; interpretability methods become statistical estimators, and findings should be tested against explicit and meaningful alternative computational hypotheses, with uncertainty quantified with respect to the postulated statistical model. It also highlights important theoretical issues, such as the identifiability of common interpretability queries, which we argue is critical to understand the field's susceptibility to false discoveries, poor generalizability, and high variance.

replace FlowSteer: Towards Agents Designing Agentic Workflows via Reinforced Progressive Canvas Editing

Authors: Mingda Zhang, Wenjin Liu, Tiesunlong Shen, Qika Lin, Rui Mao, Erik Cambria, Xiaoying Tang, Haoran Luo

Abstract: In recent years, agentic workflows have been widely applied to solve complex human tasks. However, existing workflow construction still faces key challenges, including human-dependent workflow construction, the lack of graph-level execution feedback, and the inability to repair errors in-loop during long-horizon construction. To address these challenges, we propose FlowSteer, a new paradigm of Agent Designing Agentic Workflows - a single agent itself end-to-end designs the workflow that a downstream executor runs. To support this paradigm, we introduce the Workflow Canvas, a novel executable graph-state environment that returns syntax-checked execution feedback for every atomic edit. Built on the canvas, we further propose Reinforced Progressive Canvas Editing, in which a lightweight policy agent issues one atomic edit per turn conditioned on real canvas feedback, and is trained end-to-end via reinforcement learning. Moreover, FlowSteer provides a plug-and-play framework that supports diverse operator libraries and interchangeable LLM backends. Experimental results on twelve datasets show that FlowSteer significantly outperforms baselines across various tasks. Our code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/FlowSteer-9B2E.

replace Speak or Stay Silent: Context-Aware Turn-Taking in Multi-Party Dialogue

Authors: Kratika Bhagtani (Robert), Mrinal Anand (Robert), Yu Chen (Robert), Xu, Amit Kumar Singh Yadav

Abstract: Existing voice AI assistants treat every detected pause as an invitation to speak. This works in dyadic dialogue, but in multi-party settings, where an AI assistant participates alongside multiple speakers, pauses are abundant and ambiguous. An assistant that speaks on every pause becomes disruptive rather than useful. In this work, we formulate context-aware turn-taking: at every detected pause, given the full conversation context, our method decides whether the assistant should speak or stay silent. We introduce a benchmark of over 120K labeled conversations spanning three multi-party corpora. Evaluating eight recent large language models, we find that they consistently fail at context-aware turn-taking under zero-shot prompting. We then propose a supervised fine-tuning approach with reasoning traces, improving balanced accuracy by up to 23 percentage points. Our findings suggest that context-aware turn-taking is not an emergent capability; it must be explicitly trained.

replace STAIRS-Former: Spatio-Temporal Attention with Interleaved Recursive Structure Transformer for Offline Multi-task Multi-agent Reinforcement Learning

Authors: Jiwon Jeon, Myungsik Cho, Youngchul Sung

Abstract: Offline multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) with multi-task datasets is challenging due to varying numbers of agents across tasks and the need to generalize to unseen scenarios. Prior works employ transformers with observation tokenization and hierarchical skill learning to address these issues. However, they underutilize the transformer attention mechanism for inter-agent coordination and rely on a single history token, which limits their ability to capture long-horizon temporal dependencies in partially observable MARL settings. In this paper, we propose STAIRS-Former, a transformer architecture augmented with spatial and temporal hierarchies that enables effective attention over critical tokens while capturing long interaction histories. replace Fairness of Explanations in Artificial Intelligence (AI): A Unifying Framework, Axioms, and Future Direction toward Responsible AI

Authors: Gideon Popoola, John Sheppard

Abstract: Machine learning algorithms are being used in high-stakes decisions, including those in criminal justice, healthcare, credit, and employment. The research community has responded with two largely independent research fields: \emph{algorithmic fairness}, which targets equitable outcomes, and \emph{explainable AI} (XAI), which targets interpretable reasoning. This survey identifies and maps a novel blind spot at their intersection, which is a model that can satisfy every standard fairness criterion in its outputs while being profoundly unfair in its \emph{reasoning process}. We refer to this as the procedural bias, and mitigating it requires treating the fairness of explanations as a distinct object of scientific study. To our knowledge, we provide the first unified theoretical and literature review of this emerging field and elucidate the drawbacks of post-hoc explainers in certifying explanation fairness. Our central contribution is a \emph{conditional invariance framework} formalizing explanation fairness as the requirement that explanations should be indifferent regardless of the protected attributes $ P(E(X) \in \cdot \mid X_\text{rel} = x_\text{rel},\, A = a) = P(E(X) \in \cdot \mid X_\text{rel} = x_\text{rel},\, A = b)$ for all task-relevant $x$, a single principle from which all existing explanation fairness metrics emerge as partial operationalizations. We introduce a seven-dimensional taxonomy, identify three generative mechanisms of explanation inequity (representation-driven, explanation-model mismatch, actionability-driven), and propose a canonical six-step evaluation workflow for operationalizing explanation fairness audits in practice.

replace M2A: Synergizing Mathematical and Agentic Reasoning in Large Language Models

Authors: Junjian Wang, Xin Zhou, Qiran Xu, Kun Zhan

Abstract: While reasoning has become a central capability of large language models (LLMs), the reasoning patterns required for different scenarios are often misaligned. Mathematical reasoning typically relies on intrinsic logic to solve closed-world problems in a single response, whereas agentic reasoning requires not only internal reasoning but also multi-turn interaction with external environments, interleaving thought and action. This misalignment prevents mathematical and agentic reasoning from effectively benefiting from each other, often yielding unstable reasoning behavior and only limited performance gains under multi-task learning. In this paper, we propose M2A, a novel paradigm that synergizes mathematical and agentic reasoning via model merging. To avoid overfitting to superficial reasoning patterns under joint training, M2A operates directly in parameter space: it identifies the feature subspace critical for agent behavior, and merges the mathematical reasoning task vector only along its null space, thereby injecting reasoning capability along directions that do not perturb agent behavior. Unlike SFT or RL, M2A requires no additional gradient-update and exposes the merging coefficient as a simple knob for controlling reasoning length. Experiments in a challenging real-world coding agent setting show that our method effectively extends agentic reasoning depth and delivers substantial performance improvements. Applied to a fine-tuned Qwen3-8B, M2A improves its SWE-Bench Verified resolved rate from 44.0% to 51.2% without retraining the model. Further experiments demonstrate that M2A generalizes across model families, agent scaffolds, and task domains.

replace Learning Scenario Reduction for Two-Stage Robust Optimization with Discrete Uncertainty

Authors: Tianjue Lin, Jianan Zhou, Jieyi Bi, Yaoxin Wu, Wen Song, Zhiguang Cao, Jie Zhang

Abstract: Two-Stage Robust Optimization (2RO) with discrete uncertainty is challenging, often rendering exact solutions prohibitive. Scenario reduction alleviates this issue by selecting a small, representative subset of scenarios to enable tractable computation. However, existing methods are largely problem-agnostic, operating solely on the uncertainty set without consulting the feasible region or recourse structure. In this paper, we introduce PRISE, a problem-driven sequential lookahead heuristic that constructs reduced scenario sets by evaluating the marginal impact of each scenario. While PRISE yields high-quality scenario subsets, each selection step requires solving multiple subproblems, making it computationally expensive at scale. To address this, we propose NeurPRISE, a neural surrogate model built on a GNN-Transformer backbone that encodes the per-scenario structure via graph convolution and captures cross-scenario interactions through attention. NeurPRISE is trained via imitation learning with a gain-aware ranking objective, which distills marginal gain information from PRISE into a learned scoring function for scenario ranking and selection. Extensive results on three 2RO problems show that NeurPRISE consistently achieves competitive regret relative to comprehensive methods, maintains strong calability with varying numbers of scenarios, and delivers 17-200x speedup over PRISE. NeurPRISE also exhibits strong zero-shot generalization, effectively handling instances with larger problem scales (up to 5x), more scenarios (up to 4x), and distribution shifts.

replace A Matter of TASTE: Improving Coverage and Difficulty of Agent Benchmarks

Authors: Tomer Keren, Nitay Calderon, Asaf Yehudai, Yotam Perlitz, Michal Shmueli-Scheuer, Roi Reichart

Abstract: As agent capabilities advance, existing benchmarks, such as $\tau^2$-Bench, are becoming increasingly saturated. Yet constructing new benchmark tasks remains complex, costly, and labor-intensive. Moreover, the standard approach, in which scenarios are first written in natural language and then mapped to tool sequences, captures only a narrow subset of the tool-use patterns agents exercise. In this paper, we address these problems by reversing the task construction process. We propose TASTE: Task Synthesis from Tool Sequence Evolution, an automatic method that generates challenging tasks with broader tool-use coverage. TASTE utilizes an Adaptive Contrastive $n$-gram model trained on LLM-judged validity signals. This enables sampling valid tool sequences that cover a vast range of tool combinations. TASTE then selects representative sequences from the pool via clustering, instantiates them into complete benchmark tasks, and refines them through iterative difficulty evolution. Using TASTE, we construct $\tau^c$-Bench, a challenging extension of the three domains of $\tau^2$-Bench. We evaluate $11$ agent/user LLM pairs and find that models nearly saturating $\tau^2$-Bench suffer severe performance drops on our tasks (e.g., Gemini-3-Flash falls from $0.82\!-\!0.94$ to $0.28\!-\!0.61$). Beyond increasing difficulty, our generated tasks more than double the number of unique tool combinations agents must execute. Our results suggest high scores on existing benchmarks often reflect saturation rather than robust task-solving ability. By automating the generation of difficult, high-coverage benchmarks, TASTE enables continuous, scalable evaluation of future agents.

replace Review Arcade: On the Human Alignment and Gameability of LLM Reviews

Authors: Hans Ole Hatzel, Sebastian Steindl, Jan Strich

Abstract: LLM-generated reviews for scientific papers are gaining considerable traction and are even being officially piloted by major conferences. We have to assume that not only reviewers are using LLM-assistance, but also that authors use LLMs to revise their papers before submitting. In this work, we perform empirical experiments on accepted and rejected submissions to the 2024-2025 cycles of the ACL Rolling Review (ARR) to evaluate LLM reviews from both the author and the reviewer perspective. First, we identify a limited alignment of LLM reviews with human ones. In the best-case scenario, the alignment is reasonable. However, we also find that LLM-human alignment varies substantially across prompts and models. Finally, we investigate the scenario in which the author uses an iterative draft-revise workflow to improve the submission according to the LLM review. We find that this "gaming" of LLM reviews can be effective in specific scenarios, increasing overall scores for 36% of the 984 papers.
