replace Benchmarking Open-Ended Multi-Agent Coordination in Language Agents

Authors: Kale-ab Abebe Tessera, Andras Szecsenyi, Cameron Barker, Alexander Rutherford, Davide Paglieri, Aidan Scannell, Henry Gouk, Elliot J. Crowley, Tim Rockt\"aschel, Amos Storkey

Abstract: As language models are increasingly deployed as autonomous agents, they must coordinate with others over long horizons in open-ended interactive tasks. Yet existing evaluations rarely test these demands together, focusing instead on single-agent tasks, short interactions, or highly structured multi-agent settings. We introduce $alem$, a JAX-based benchmark for open-ended multi-agent coordination built on Craftax-like dynamics. Alem embeds procedurally generated coordination tasks, soft specialisation, communication, and controllable coordination difficulty into a long-horizon survival world with exploration, crafting, trading, and combat. We evaluate $13$ LLMs zero-shot within homogeneous teams, with trained MARL agents as reference points. Current LLM agents remain far from solving alem, averaging only ~6% normalised return, but their failures are not uniform. On the hardest coordination setting, zero-shot Gemini-3.1-Pro-High approaches MARL agents trained for one billion steps, while GPT-5.4-High achieves strong base-task reward but much lower coordination reward. This contrast shows that individual task competence does not imply coordination competence. Further experiments reveal that communication is the largest contributor to coordination, while memory and reasoning help when used to maintain multi-step plans. In heterogeneous teams, however, combining different models improves base-task progress but lowers coordination reward relative to their homogeneous counterparts. Overall, our results identify coordination as a distinct bottleneck for frontier LLM agents, separate from single-agent capabilities. Alem makes this bottleneck measurable and provides a controlled testbed for developing agents that communicate, allocate roles, and execute shared plans. Code is available at https://github.com/alem-world/alem-env, with LLM traces and a leaderboard at https://alem-world.github.io.

URLs: https://github.com/alem-world/alem-env,, https://alem-world.github.io.

replace FlowR2A: Learning Reward-to-Action Distribution for Multimodal Driving Planning

Authors: Xirui Li, Zhe Liu, Xiaoqing Ye, Wenhua Han, Yifeng Pan, Junyu Han, Hengshuang Zhao

Abstract: Multimodal driving planning faces a long-standing tension between two paradigms: scoring-based methods benefit from dense reward supervision but are confined to a fixed action vocabulary, while anchor-based methods generate proposals dynamically yet suffer from sparse supervision constrained to a single ground-truth trajectory. In this work, we propose FlowR2A, which resolves this tension by reframing simulation-based rewards from discriminative targets into generative conditions. By learning the reward-conditioned action distribution from dense trajectory-reward pairs with a flow-matching decoder, FlowR2A unifies the dense supervision of scoring-based methods with the proposal generation of anchor-based methods in a single generative model, forcing the model to internalize the correlation between an action and its outcomes in safety, progress, comfort, and rule compliance. To balance hard safety constraints against soft progress objectives, we introduce fine-grained per-timestep reward conditioning and reward noise augmentation. The generative formulation naturally supports controllable test-time sampling via reward guidance and anchored sampling, producing high-quality proposals. FlowR2A achieves state-of-the-art results on the NAVSIM v1 and v2 benchmarks, with multimodal proposals of substantially higher quality than prior methods.

replace SkillBoost: A Constrained Exploration-Exploitation Process for Mitigating Skill Overfitting

Authors: Hongqiang Lin, Chao Liu, Xiaofan Bai, Xuan Jin, Yuhong Li, Nenggan Zheng, Xipeng Cao

Abstract: Enabling large language model (LLM) agents to accumulate and reuse experience from past interactions remains a central challenge in real-world applications. A promising solution is to treat skills as trainable states and optimize them in the same way as model parameters in neural network training. However, data-driven skill optimization is prone to overfitting to the limited trajectories collected from real environments. Overexploiting these trajectories overfits the current batch, while unconstrained exploration causes regression on previously solved cases. This tension motivates a constrained search view of skill self-evolution, governed by an exploration--exploitation trade-off. We propose SkillBoost, a three-stage framework that mitigates both risks: structured exploitation localizes observed failures to editable skill components, prior-guided exploration draws on prior knowledge in the LLM to generate diverse repair candidates, and verified acceptance commits a candidate only when it improves performance within a regression bound. Experiments across 23 model--benchmark configurations show that SkillBoost achieves state-of-the-art performance while mitigating overfitting, outperforming both human-crafted and LLM-generated skills. Notably, it matches or even surpasses the performance gains typically achieved through post-training. Furthermore, transfer experiments further show that optimized skills can be reused by other agents on similar tasks. The code is available at https://github.com/HQ-Lin/SkillBoost

URLs: https://github.com/HQ-Lin/SkillBoost

replace Risky Business: Measuring The Faithfulness-Safety Tension

Authors: Dominik Meier, Luca Joshua Francis, Marco Bernhard Kaiser, Terry Ruas, Jan Philip Wahle, Bela Gipp

Abstract: Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning offers a promising window into model monitoring. However, monitoring relies on faithfulness, i.e., the model output strictly derives from its reasoning trace. We identify an alignment tension where a model must be faithful enough to be monitored, yet robust enough to reject unsafe reasoning. We demonstrate that this counterbalance exists in current Large Reasoning Models (LRMs), and show ways in which it can be addressed. We introduce HazMart, a human-written dataset set in an autonomous AI shopkeeper scenario. Unlike prior work that relies on providing hints in prompts to test faithfulness (e.g., "A Stanford professor said it should be Answer A"), we propose a novel replacement-based technique, which we call Targeted Reasoning Replacement (TRR), that directly intervenes in the reasoning chain to substitute in unsafe or illogical thoughts (e.g., "Wait, the answer must be Option B [was Option A] because it is the most fitting"). DeepSeek-R1-Llama-70B exhibits high faithfulness (97.5%) but fails to reject Unsafe Reasoning (12.3%), while QwQ-32B is more robust (73.9% safety) at the cost of lower faithfulness (74.7%). Mechanistic analyses of QwQ-32B reveal that these properties are represented by anti-correlated internal directions peaking at the action-commit token. Finally, we demonstrate that representation steering can independently amplify the safety direction, increasing safe behavior by 9 percentage points while maintaining base capabilities.

replace The Transformer Revolution, Part 1: Dynamic Processing through Output-Weight Interconnections

Authors: Marco Giunti, Fabrizia Giulia Garavaglia

Abstract: We reinterpret Transformer inference by developing a functionally equivalent mechanical-structural description of its functional architecture. Parameterized transformations of token representations are identified with simple neural networks organized through output-input and output-weight interconnections. This reconstruction makes explicit a constitutive feature of ordinary Transformer inference: outputs generated from the current input determine the weights, and hence the transformations, of other networks. We show that the resulting mechanical-structural organization is functionally equivalent to the standard matrix description and gives rise to Sequence-level Interactive Dynamic Parallel Processing (SIDPP). We then quantify the dynamic component of inference. For a fixed Transformer architecture, the number of dynamic parameters generated by a prompt grows linearly with prompt length, with a proportionality constant determined by the model architecture, whereas the number of static parameters remains constant. We call the resulting structural dependence of processing on the prompt strong prompt sensitivity. GPT-4.5's recent Turing test success provides a striking behavioral phenomenon for which this framework offers a viable explanatory route. Finally, we discuss implications for interpretability, control, predictability, and neuromorphic construction. We argue that known biological mechanisms support the in-principle neural constructibility of a form of SIDPP, and on this basis formulate Conjecture T as a conditional research hypothesis concerning cortical language processing.

replace DiSCO: Defending text-to-image generation through distribution-guided contrastive prompt optimization

Authors: Tong Zhang, Motasem Alfarra, Carlos Hinojosa, Christos Louizos, Bernard Ghanem

Abstract: As text-to-image generative models advance, they raise critical safety concerns, particularly the generation of Not-Safe-For-Work (NSFW) content such as violence and nudity, further exacerbated by red-teaming adversarial attacks. Existing defenses predominantly operate under white-box assumptions, relying on text encoder optimization, weight editing, or inference-time intervention, and fundamentally cannot scale to proprietary models. Black-box alternatives based on LLM prompt rewriting offer broader applicability, yet fail in a critical regime we identify as the \textit{benign adversarial} problem: prompts that are linguistically safe but still trigger harmful generation due to the model's learned data distribution. We propose DiSCO, a zero-shot, strictly black-box defense that operates entirely at the prompt level as a plug-and-play module, requiring no model retraining, fine-tuning, or access to model internals. DiSCO performs distribution-guided suffix expansion via beam search, optimized through contrastive scoring over safe and unsafe image pools generated by the target model itself, with iterative adaptive feedback until safe content is produced. We demonstrate that DiSCO consistently enhances the safety of both undefended and defended models on the I2P benchmark under multiple red-teaming attacks, achieving 37.7% and 25.13% ASR reduction, respectively, while maintaining semantic fidelity and improving image coherence. As a black-box, architecture-agnostic module, DiSCO can be readily applied to any text-to-image system without necessitating any changes to the model itself.

replace Depth Enables Local Entropy: Quadratic Depth Dependence in Deep Variation-Norm ReLU Regression

Authors: Tao Jiang, Minbo Gao, Shaowei Cai

Abstract: How small can a layer-sum variation budget be while deep ReLU regression keeps a statistical complexity quadratic in depth? We study Gaussian regression on the circle over compositions of $L$ vector-valued ReLU blocks of width $w$, whose second-order variation costs sum to at most $A$. With unit output bound and noise level, a budget $CLw^\varepsilon$ already forces minimax risk $\Omega(\varepsilon^2\min\{1,L^2w^2\log w/n\})$ for each fixed $\varepsilon\in(0,2]$ and $L\gtrsim1/\varepsilon$. This is far below the budget of order $Lw^2$ needed when every layer carries the full gain. When $L\gtrsim\log w$, a budget $CL$ forces risk $\Omega(\min\{1,L^2w^2/(n\log w)\})$, and over a wide range of sample sizes no budget below a small multiple of $L$ reaches this risk. Conversely, every function in the class has derivative variation at most $2(A/L)^L$, independently of the width, and in a low-budget regime the minimax risk matches that of a one-dimensional second-variation ball of radius $(A/L)^L$. The budget thus moves the class from the behavior of a one-dimensional smoothness ball to statistical complexity quadratic in depth, and a budget linear in depth already suffices for the latter.

replace A Table Is Worth 64 Tokens: Pixel-level Compression for Multi-Table Document Question Answering

Authors: I\~nigo Alonso, Mirella Lapata

Abstract: Answering questions over real-world documents requires processing long inputs that interleave text with tables. Optical context compression, which represents context as images, promises to reduce token cost, but its effect on table understanding remains unclear. We study pixel-level table compression for question answering over documents with multiple tables, evaluating seven VLMs across two benchmarks and five visual-token budgets. At moderate budgets, table images match html in accuracy with fewer tokens, but aggressive downscaling makes models reason longer without recovering accuracy, eroding the savings. Yet even at 64 visual tokens per table, tables preserve enough signal to identify whether they are relevant to a question. We exploit this asymmetry with a training-free, two-stage method: the model first identifies the tables needed to answer a question from a pixel-compressed context, and then reasons over those tables at native resolution. On long financial documents, it uses 41% fewer tokens than single-step QA over native-resolution tables while gaining 5 accuracy points, and 21% fewer than the most efficient compressed configuration at no accuracy cost. It also still saves tokens when applied to retrieved context.

replace Do Latent World Models Really Select the Right Actions?

Authors: Zhengshu Zhang, Zhiyuan Li

Abstract: This paper shows that the planning success of a latent world model does not reveal how well its score selects actions. Latent world models score candidate action sequences by the distance between their predicted embeddings and the embedding of a goal image, and their strong planning results are naturally read as evidence that this score picks good actions. Our study is simple: we take two released models, JEPA-WM and DINO-WM, execute the action sequences their planners consider, and compare what the score prefers with what actually happens. We make two observations. First, the score is informative but unreliable: it picks better actions than chance, yet it often passes over a successful sequence that the search has already found. Second, planning still succeeds, helped by a wider search and by replanning from new observations. With the released search budget, PointMaze planning succeeds in 91% of episodes, yet the top-scored candidate at the end of a search is no more likely to succeed than a random one. These observations suggest that the score should be measured directly, next to the success rate.

replace Support Topology and Gradient Mixing in Sinkhorn Layers

Authors: Dylan Forde

Abstract: Sinkhorn attention layers normalize weights over a fixed sparse mask by alternating row and column scaling. When training these layers, sparse masks are usually judged by their edge count, graph diameter, or forward reachability. We show that the sparsity pattern and marginal budgets also govern how gradients flow backward through the scaling steps. Differentiating one row-column cycle on column log-potentials yields an explicit row-stochastic Markov kernel built from the two half-step transport plans, which reduces to a reversible two-step random walk at a balanced fixed point. Because potentials are defined only up to additive constants on each connected component, reverse-mode gradients propagate as zero-sum vectors whose total-variation norm contracts at a rate given by the kernel's Dobrushin coefficient. This coefficient bounds both unrolled backpropagation and implicit-differentiation truncation error. Crucially, graph connectivity alone does not prevent gradient trapping: we prove a face-lattice dichotomy showing that a mask and marginals guarantee score-uniform one-step contraction if and only if every nonempty face of the transportation polytope retains pairwise two-hop column overlap; otherwise, large learned scores can push the contraction rate arbitrarily close to one. We extend this criterion to multi-layer schedules, prove exact one-sweep mixing for coordinate-factorized and butterfly masks, and derive closed-form bounds for expanders, local bands, and global hub tokens. Deterministic numerical checks verify our finite-difference adjoints, canonical sparse masks, adversarial score limits, and spectral formulas.

replace Exploring Diffusion Transformers for Cross-Modal Augmentation in Multimodal Brain State Decoding

Authors: Ziwei Wang, Xingyi He, Hongbin Wang, Tianwang Jia, Bohan Fang, Dongrui Wu

Abstract: Multimodal brain state decoding has largely focused on fusing paired modalities for prediction, but has rarely explored how their correspondence can be further exploited to enrich training data and improve multimodal representation learning. To address this gap, we propose CoMA-DiT, a bidirectional cross-modal Diffusion Transformer for latent augmentation that treats paired modalities as sources of mutual generative supervision rather than merely as inputs to be fused. CoMA-DiT conditions velocity prediction on the paired modality through cross-modal attention and uses the shared hidden representation to construct reliability-gated residual augmentations. Experiments on multimodal auditory attention decoding and emotion recognition showed that CoMA-DiT consistently outperformed more than 20 representative baselines, achieving absolute gains of 4.28 and 6.70 percentage points in accuracy and macro-F1 over the no-augmentation baseline, respectively. Extensive ablation, sensitivity, visualization, and interpretability analyses further demonstrated its robustness, generalizability, and ability to capture functionally relevant cross-modal interactions. These findings support a broader view of multimodal learning: Paired modalities can serve not only as inputs for fusion but also as supervision sources that augment one another.

replace Designer-RSI: Evolving Procedural Memory from User Traffic for Graphic Design Agent

Authors: Hongyang Du, Lan Yan, Christian Flores, Asim Kadav

Abstract: Deployed software agents face a continuous stream of user requests while their foundation-model weights remain frozen between releases. Recursive self-improvement (RSI) via external memory offers a practical path to continual adaptation, yet prior methods largely assume programmatic verifiers, cheap retries, or a single global prompt. Creative software breaks these assumptions, as open-ended visual briefs lack ground truth, VLM judges are noisy, and multi-step tool executions are costly and confounded by external assets. We present Designer-RSI, which evolves a modular skill bank from unlabeled design trajectories for an agent spanning over 230 raster, vector, and layout tools. Designer-RSI couples widening (acquiring new skills for recurring capability gaps) with deepening (revising failing skills by contrasting successful and failed runs), and screens updates through a Pareto-style matched replay gate that requires strict gains on sampled briefs without local regressions. Across held-out product briefs, eight external benchmarks, and two backbones, traffic-driven evolution outperforms static documentation skills, lifts execution success up to 99.8%, improves requirement fulfillment, and reaches up to a 67.6% blind win rate over the base agent. Ablations further show that skill acquisition and revision are strongly synergistic: neither suffices alone, and reliable gains require evolving coverage and execution quality together.

replace Few-Shot Demonstrations Elicit the Use of In-Context World Representations in LLMs

Authors: Kohsei Matsutani, Gouki Minegishi, Core Francisco Park, Takeshi Kojima, Yusuke Iwasawa, Yutaka Matsuo

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs), when acting as agents, are expected to take observed data in context, infer the latent state space underlying the world, and leverage it for downstream prediction. However, prior work demonstrated that LLMs struggle to use representations learned in context on a graph tracking task, where the model needs to construct a representation of the graph governing data generation process and use it for subsequent predictions. In this paper, we show that extending this to few-shot settings, where each demonstration is generated from a different world with either the same or different graph topologies, enhances its prediction on 9 models from 7 model families. To understand this improvement, we linearly probe a low-dimensional world representation that encodes graph information in the hidden states. Notably, we find that few-shot demonstrations relocate the world representation and increase its predictive use. Specifically, for each model, these world representations shift in directions nearly orthogonal to their original subspace, and interventions on these representations selectively impair performance more than interventions on other subspaces. Consistent with this insight, we show that few-shot demonstrations with observations from different worlds improve performance on ARC-AGI-1&2, web agent tasks, and Othello. Our findings elucidate the role and internal mechanisms of few-shot demonstrations in in-context world modeling and provide implications for their further improvement.

replace Diagnosing and Improving Probabilistic Reasoning in Large Language Models

Authors: Huaman Sun, Dingcheng Wang, Jason Hartline, Jessica Hullman

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly proposed as decision assistants who must reason probabilistically from available evidence under explicit decision costs. We propose a decision-theoretic framework that decomposes LLMs' decision loss into two components: forming accurate beliefs from provided evidence and translating those beliefs into actions that optimize a provided utility function. Using a synthetic benchmark with known ground truth, we apply the decomposition to characterize probabilistic reasoning in frontier and open-sourced models. We further evaluate whether RL interventions targeting beliefs, decisions, or both improve these components across three domains, whether improvements transfer across components and elicitation formats, and whether decision performance can improve without improvement in belief formation. We find that targeting one component of probabilistic reasoning improves the target without necessarily transferring to others, and that jointly targeting belief formation and decision-making improves both but hinges on matched formats between training and evaluation.

replace Keep It CALM: Analyzing the Limits of Global Unsafety in Text-to-Image Generation

Authors: NaHyeon Park, Minhyun Lee, Hyunjung Shim

Abstract: Training-free safeguards for text-to-image generation often rely on a reusable safety signal, such as an unsafe direction or global toxic subspace, applied broadly across prompts. We provide a controlled geometric analysis of this global-unsafety assumption and reveal a consistent coverage-selectivity trade-off: compact unsafe subspaces fail to cover heterogeneous unsafe semantics, whereas broader aggregation increasingly distorts safety-adjacent benign prompts. Motivated by this finding, we propose CALM (Counterfactual Adaptive Local Modulation), a training-free safeguard that replaces uniform global removal with prompt-local counterfactual correction. Using matched unsafe-benign anchors, CALM routes each prompt to active unsafe categories, minimally edits only violating token representations toward the safe side, and suppresses positively aligned unsafe residual components. Across broad evaluation, CALM significantly improves unsafe content suppression while preserving benign utility, demonstrating that local counterfactual correction provides a more selective alternative to global unsafe signal removal.

replace InvestigationWorlds: An Agentic Environment for Legal Investigation

Authors: Albert Yu Sun, Andrew Benard, Sil Hamilton, Anna Teresita A. Marcelo, Yong Jae Kim, Carl-Leander Henneking, Rundong Hu, Yuhong Wang, David Mimno, Bishan Yang, Igor Labutov

Abstract: We introduce InvestigationWorlds, an agentic environment for legal investigation. We build on an underused artifact of U.S. civil litigation: the summary judgment motion. This motion relies upon a record composed of real evidence exhibits, and results in a court-adopted hypothesis that is treated as ground truth for the purposes of deciding the motion. replace CADFather: Autonomous CAD Reconstruction through Coordinated Tool Use

Authors: Gennadiy Savrasov, Maksim Elistratov, Nikita Gavrilov, Albert Garifullin, Oleg Pavlov, Soslan Kabisov, Vladimir Frolov, Anton Konushin, Andrey Kuznetsov, Dmitrii Zhemchuzhnikov

Abstract: Reconstructing an editable CAD model from a 3D shape remains a challenging engineering task. Existing methods can propose CAD operations, but no single source of proposals works equally well across different part geometries and stages of reconstruction. We introduce CADFather, an autonomous agentic system that coordinates complementary tools to recover parametric CAD programs from 3D meshes. A vision-language assistant inspects renders of the target and intermediate reconstructions, then decides which candidate CAD programs to extend, which tools to invoke, how many proposals to generate, and when to finish. Proposed or refined programs are executed and evaluated to provide feedback for subsequent decisions. The agent maintains alternative candidate programs for each target part and preserves the best valid result throughout reconstruction. CADFather uses pretrained generation and assistant models without additional training. We evaluate reconstruction quality and execution validity on the full DeepCAD, Fusion360, and MCB test sets, as well as on CADENA-Bench, CADBench, and BenchCAD. Abstract: Computer-aided retrosynthesis has progressed through single-step prediction, tree search and route scoring, yet complex targets still require route-level judgement where stereochemistry, functional-group compatibility, cost and greenness interact. replace Beyond Static Leaderboards: Capability-Focused Evaluation with Structural Diversity for Time Series Foundation Models

Authors: Hongyin Zhang, Mingyi Xiang, Rongzhao Chen, Yankai Wang, Jialin Qiao, Xiangdong Huang, Xiaoou Ding, Chen Wang, Zhongyi Pei, Jianmin Wang

Abstract: Static public benchmarks for time series foundation models (TSFMs) typically have fixed and narrow test distributions. Their repeated reuse encourages benchmark-specific tuning, making reported performance reflect adaptation to the fixed test set rather than genuine forecasting generalization. In addition, a single aggregate score cannot fully characterize model capabilities across distinct temporal structures and input relationships. We propose CaFE (Capability-Focused Evaluation), a refreshable evaluation extension grounded in original benchmark data that enriches static benchmarks with structural diversity and enriches single-score black-box evaluation with multi-dimensional, capability-oriented assessment. CaFE first profiles the original benchmark and defines eight Structural Features (SFs) that characterize its temporal structure. For each SF, it resamples the original data according to its structural support, removes edge-of-distribution samples, and augments the retained samples by varying the SF's salience, producing samples with different structural profiles. After filtering out out-of-distribution samples while preserving diversity, CaFE yields a refreshed evaluation set. TSFMs are then evaluated on this set using both conventional forecast-error metrics and our proposed intervention-response metrics, giving each model a per-feature capability profile alongside endpoint accuracy. Experiments on GIFT-Eval and FEV-Mini20 show that CaFE yields stable evaluation results and reveals per-feature strengths and weaknesses that a single aggregate score hides. Moreover, on genuine data filtered by structural condition, models selected by CaFE outperform those chosen by the aggregate leaderboard. CaFE therefore makes model selection conditional on the structural response of interest.

replace PEAR: Adaptive Advantage Reshaping for Perception and Exploration in Large Vision-Language Models

Authors: Junxin Wang, Yingfeng Luo, Xiangchen Song, Weiqiao Shan, Runsong Zhao, Xinyu Liu, Shunjie Xing, Yifu Huo, Dingyang Lin, Qiuyu Gu, Yongyu Mu, Chenglong Wang, Changliang Li, Jingbo Zhu, Tong Xiao

Abstract: Effective multimodal reasoning requires a model to reliably utilize visual information while sufficiently exploring diverse reasoning paths. replace Learnings and Pitfalls from AI-Assisted A/B Experimentation at Scale

Authors: Brett Daley, Ilya Gorbachev, Anubhav Natani, Nicholas Brown, Athena Dai, Rachel Song, Marcin Pekalski, Josip Domazet, Anchal Choubey, Daniel Ting

Abstract: A/B testing underpins product decisions by providing trustworthy, reproducible quantification of proposed changes' effects. Growing large language model (LLM) capabilities have motivated efforts to automate experimentation workflows. Yet LLMs are nondeterministic and prone to hallucination, properties that conflict with the trust experimentation systems must provide. Experiments are also operationally messy: diagnosing anomalies, debugging configurations, and conducting bespoke follow-up analyses require flexible reasoning and manual effort. These tasks let agents accelerate experimentation without replacing its deterministic statistical foundations. We report lessons from building and operating such an agent. Our central finding is that this tension is resolved not by making the model deterministic, but by bounding where its nondeterminism can reach; the model selects and sequences procedures, while deterministic code computes every quantity it reports. Three consequences surprised us: withholding information from the model improved its answers, demand concentrated on mundane retrieval rather than the autonomous decisions we anticipated, and the agent's eagerness to help threatened statistical validity.

replace RegimeCouncil: Paired-Evidence Routing For Memory Conflict Governance In LLM Agents

Authors: Qingcan Kang, Mingyang Liu, Shixiong Kai, Tao Zhong, Mingxuan Yuan

Abstract: Alongside memory storage and retrieval, a fundamental challenge in long-term LLM agent systems is how the agent should act when stored memories conflict with an evolving world. Facts often go stale without explicit deletion, so the conflict surfaces before the store is refreshed: delayed feedback shows that acting on one outdated record hurt, but not whether suppression should stay on that fact (a local policy) or extend to other stored facts (a global policy). Existing systems hard-code one of these scopes at design time. We formulate this online choice, under hidden validity and delayed feedback, as memory conflict governance, and propose RegimeCouncil, a lightweight router that keeps both scopes as advisors and arbitrates between them without retraining the LLM. Each memory-assisted response is scored against a no-memory anchor, by off-path paired comparison or by inverse-propensity estimation from the executed action alone. A Local Advisor tracks only the queried fact, a Global Advisor pools recent outcomes across the store, and an asymmetric Meta Governor follows Local by default, switching to Global only when pooling yields a strictly positive recent utility margin. We prove that using delayed outcomes escapes the prompt-only regret floor, that neither fixed scope suffices alone, and that this margin gate switches within bounded time. On LongMemEval streams, historical drift in TempLAMA and TAQA, delays $\tau \in [1, 96]$, and sparse or bandit feedback, with Qwen3-30B-A3B and GPT-4o, RegimeCouncil outperforms the better fixed scope in all 30 held-out settings, by 5.2-7.3 points on disagreement episodes and +27.9 points over standalone cold-start fallback under clustered shifts, while cutting harmful stale-memory use at microsecond overhead.

replace When Safety Rationales Hallucinate: Diagnosing and Grounding Multimodal Safety Guards

Authors: Yingshuo Wang, Weixiang Zhao, Guancheng Li, Jiahe Guo, Yu Zhang, Yanyan Zhao

Abstract: Multimodal safety guards increasingly generate rationales alongside safety predictions, yet whether these rationales are reliable remains largely unexplored. We systematically study *rationale hallucination* from two perspectives: visual rationale hallucination, where rationales contain unsupported visual facts, and safety attribution hallucination, where grounded content is assigned an incorrect safety interpretation. Across rationale-generation methods, multiple vision-language backbones, and safety datasets, we find that rationale hallucination is widespread and does not consistently diminish with higher classification accuracy or increased model scale. Further analysis of the Think-with-Image paradigm shows that hallucination is concentrated in fine-grained attributes and relations, while visual tools are often invoked infrequently or without sufficient spatial focus. Based on these findings, we propose *Tool-Contrastive Grounding Reinforcement Learning* (TCG-RL), which rewards tool use only when it improves rationale grounding over a tool-free reference. TCG-RL substantially reduces rationale hallucination while maintaining competitive safety prediction performance. By revealing the gap between prediction correctness and rationale reliability, our study motivates a shift in multimodal safety guarding from outcome-only evaluation toward safety decisions that are both accurate and verifiably grounded.

replace MixFold: Sub-4-Bit Mixed-Precision LLM Inference via Output-Channel Grouping on Mobile NPUs

Authors: Vinnam Kim, Seong Hoon Seo, Junhan Kim, Jaeseong You, An Chen

Abstract: Sub-4-bit quantization allows mobile devices to run larger LLMs, but its memory savings do not always translate into faster decoding on mobile NPUs. Their matrix units support only a few native integer datatypes, and converting unsupported weight formats on the vector unit can erase the gains from fewer bits. Mixed precision faces an additional constraint: each matrix-unit tile uses one datatype. MixFold executes 3-bit and perfectly symmetric 2-bit weights using native integer operations without expanding the weights to wider datatypes during decoding. MixFold then assigns datatypes to output-channel groups aligned with the matrix-unit tiles, enabling finer-than-layer assignment without disrupting matrix-unit execution. We compare MixFold with llama-cpp Hexagon backend on the Qualcomm Hexagon NPU of a Snapdragon 8 Elite Gen 5. Across the Qwen3 model family, MixFold achieves comparable or higher accuracy than llama-cpp's sub-4-bit formats at matched bits per weight, while decoding 1.16-1.41 times faster and generating 1.5-4.3 times more tokens per joule.

replace From Acting to Learning: Experience Selection toward Continually Learning Agents

Authors: Yaoyuan Yan, Haoxiang Jie, Zhiyou Heng, Kailin Wang, Yumiao Li, Lifeng Liu, Anwan Yan

Abstract: We study how selection rules and coverage constraints affect what a 4B language agent learns from successful interactions. The comparison covers nine selectors, two fixed experience pools, eight initialization seeds, and two sampling seeds, producing 288 trained students. Each student is evaluated on 128 development task groups with the same executor. With equal global selection freedom, 256 distinct contents, and 768 training presentations, optimizer-aware gradient selection reaches 81.45\% and 73.19\% success in the two pools. It improves on global random selection by 8.25 and 5.96 percentage points, and on global high-NLL selection by 4.10 and 4.05 points. All eight initialization-level contrasts are positive against both baselines in each pool. Relaxing task/action quotas improves gradient selection but lowers random-selection performance: the benefit of selection freedom depends on the ranking rule. A separately frozen replication on 128 reserved task groups is complete; gradient selection gains +7.03 points over random and +5.03 over high NLL after averaging the two pools equally.

replace EvoViM: Recursively Self-Evolving Visual Agents for Exploration with Deferred Queries

Authors: Xinyu Cao, Tianjie Ju, Zhuosheng Zhang

Abstract: Multimodal large language model (MLLM) agents have shown promising capabilities in task-oriented environments. However, they still struggle when queries are deferred until after exploration, requiring them to retain visual evidence before knowing which details will matter. We introduce EvoViM, a recursively self-evolving framework in which a frozen multimodal model turns its own interaction experience into one visual program for memory and skills. The program retains images and computes directly on pixels, and its operations decide how this evidence is used in each task. During memory evolution, the same model traces failures in its visual trajectories to the operations that lost or misused evidence and revises them. It also rewrites the notes and analysis tools that guide later revisions, which then build on earlier findings and skip changes that have already failed. We evaluate EvoViM with three multimodal models on six tasks covering visual matching, spatial recall, and sequential decision-making, including three tasks with deferred queries. Across the six tasks, EvoViM improves the average score over AutoMem, the strongest baseline in the main comparison, by 31.3-47.6 points on held-out episodes. Programs written once from the initial environment instructions perform substantially worse, highlighting the value of interaction experience in shaping how visual evidence is retained and used. Code and dataset are available at https://evovim.github.io/.

URLs: https://evovim.github.io/.

Authors: Dexia Chen, YiLong Hu, Wenqi Zhong, Guangyu Guo, Tony C. W. Mok, Ling Zhang, Zhongwei Qiu

Abstract: High-stakes vertical medical tasks, such as postoperative pathological staging and long-term survival prediction, demand multi-step clinical reasoning over heterogeneous evidence under weak supervision and high outcome uncertainty. While we first orchestrate CliAgent, a domain-tailored clinical agent workflow that systematically grounds predictions in multi-modal patient evidence, manually designed medical agents remain constrained by static, brittle rules. Moreover, existing recursive self-improvement (RSI) methods suffer from feedback ambiguity and severe development-set overfitting when evolving agents in high-noise medical domains. Focusing on improving the improver, we propose Semantic PID-RSI, an evidence-guided control-theoretic framework for medical agent self-improvement. Inspired by PID control, our improver evaluates candidate modification batches via three diagnostic roles: P isolates current clinical failure modes, I synthesizes longitudinal evidence from past trials, and D estimates response dynamics and regression risks (D-pre/D-post). Selected plans are compiled into behavior contracts for staged probing and independent adoption judging. Instantiated and validated on challenging oncology tasks (pT/pN staging and survival prognosis), evolving CliAgent with Semantic PID-RSI on a 3B-activated open-weight model achieves substantial gains (up to +6.27 C-index points), outperforming overfitting-prone RSI baselines and approaching the performance of frontier proprietary models (e.g., GPT-6 series) equipped with CliAgent.

replace Differential Proteomics to Symbolic Rules: An Audit of SHAP Guidance in Lung Adenocarcinoma

Authors: Surajit Das

Abstract: We tested whether SHAP improves protein-based Horn rules for distinguishing lung adenocarcinoma from adjacent-normal tissue. CPTAC supplied 213 samples (111 tumour, 102 adjacent-normal) from 111 patients, including 102 matched pairs. Per-protein effects and tests used the same complete pairs with Benjamini-Hochberg correction. Sparse logistic regression used five stratified patient-grouped outer folds, with preprocessing and tuning confined to training data and feature selection repeated within inner folds. A separate five-fold patient-grouped evaluation of inductive logic programming used training-derived evidence and discretisation. Of 9,518 proteins passing quality control, 378 differed in abundance (40 up, 338 down) at FDR below 0.05 and an absolute mean paired log-ratio difference of at least 1. Classifier ROC-AUC, average precision, accuracy and balanced accuracy were 0.9997, 0.9998, 0.9906 and 0.9906, respectively. On the original held-out rule splits, accuracy and F1 were 0.9765 and 0.9774, respectively. One clause appeared in two folds and the others appeared once. On common folds across all 111 patients, F1 was 0.9600 with SHAP, 0.9910 without SHAP and 0.9909 for L1 logistic regression. The inclusive SHAP cutoff retained all 100 ranked proteins per audited fold. Absolute logistic-regression coefficients reproduced the SHAP-guided candidate sets, clauses and held-out predictions in both the all-patient and paired-only analyses. A single-protein threshold reproduced full-rule predictions in the paired-only cohort. SHAP guidance did not improve held-out F1 on the common folds. Diagnostic utility, early detection, prognosis and generalisability beyond this cohort remain unestablished. Biomarker and clinical claims require independent validation, rule-stability testing under repeated resampling and further biological annotation.

replace mini-psych-agent: Evaluating Human-Like Behavior in LLM Agents through Interactive Psychological Environments

Authors: Yi-Fei Cao, Tianxin Huang, Hanbo Xie, Jian-Qiao Zhu

Abstract: A Large Language Model (LLM), its harness, and the test-time compute budget jointly determine its behavior. Consequently, the same underlying model, embedded in different agentic workflows or allocated different amounts of test-time compute, can exhibit markedly different performance. Psychologists face an analogous challenge when evaluating human performance: the same person may behave differently under different incentives and levels of deliberation. Psychological tasks therefore provide an ideal testbed for studying such behavioral variation. However, when adapted to LLMs, these tasks are typically text-transcribed, replaying fixed, language-rendered experiments as static prompts. This format is ill-suited to agent evaluation, where actions can alter future observations and task states. Conversely, although dynamic environments are prevalent in the evaluation of LLM agents, they rarely ask whether the resulting behavior is human-like. Here, we bridge this gap by transforming psychological tasks into dynamic environments in which agents can interact and by using a learned human-policy model to evaluate how compatible the resulting behavioral trajectories are with human behavior. Across these environments, we find that this compatibility varies with an LLM's agentic configuration and the psychological task, and that memory raises it mainly by making choices more predictable rather than more human-like. These findings motivate a more holistic evaluation of LLM agents in psychology-inspired environments, accounting for both the backbone model and its agentic scaffold.

replace Learning Multimodal Representations from Partially Paired, Small-Scale Data

Authors: Merel Kuijs, Till Richter, Rushin H. Gindra, Korbinian Traeuble, Christian Matek, Rebeka Lukn\'arov\'a, Tingying Peng, Fabian J. Theis

Abstract: Modeling multimodal data from partially paired samples is critical for advancing domains like biomedicine, where vast unimodal datasets and foundation models exist but paired data remains scarce. Existing fusion methods rely on large-scale paired datasets, limiting their use in scenarios with incomplete pairing. We introduce CAMEO, an adversarial learning-based modality fusion framework that integrates modalities from small, partially paired datasets. Combining any pre-trained unimodal encoder with a cross-modal latent alignment mechanism, CAMEO learns shared representations requiring only minimal paired samples. To facilitate further research, we additionally release a fully annotated HuggingFace dataset comprising three organs and paired image and gene expression modalities. By extending fusion methods to address limited pairing and small-scale datasets, we provide a framework that advances multimodal learning and broadens its applicability to real-world biomedical problems.

replace-cross HindSight: A Graph-Based Vision Model Architecture For Representing Part-Whole Hierarchies

Authors: Muhammad AbdurRafae

Abstract: This paper presents a model architecture for encoding the representations of part-whole hierarchies in images in form of a graph. The idea is to divide the image into patches of different levels and then treat all of these patches as nodes for a fully connected graph. A dynamic feature extraction module is used to extract feature representations from these patches in each graph iteration. This enables us to learn a rich graph representation of the image that encompasses the inherent part-whole hierarchical information. Utilizing proper self-supervised training techniques, such a model can be trained as a general purpose vision encoder model which can then be used for various vision related downstream tasks (e.g., Image Classification, Object Detection, Image Captioning, etc.).

replace-cross The necessity of depth for artificial neural networks to approximate certain classes of smooth and bounded functions without the curse of dimensionality

Authors: Lukas Gonon, Robin Graeber, Arnulf Jentzen

Abstract: In this article we study high-dimensional approximation capacities of shallow and deep artificial neural networks (ANNs) with the rectified linear unit (ReLU) activation. In particular, it is a key contribution of this work to reveal that for all $a\in\mathbb{R}$, $b\in(a,\infty)$ with $\max\{\lvert a\rvert,\lvert b\rvert\}>1$ we have that the functions $[a,b]^d\ni x=(x_1,\ldots,x_d)\mapsto\prod_{i=1}^d x_i\in\mathbb{R}$ for $d\in\mathbb{N}=\{1,2,3,\ldots\}$ as well as the functions $[a,b]^d\ni x=(x_1,\ldots,x_d)\mapsto\sin(\prod_{i=1}^d x_i)\in\mathbb{R}$ for $d\in\mathbb{N}$ can neither be approximated without the curse of dimensionality by means of shallow ANNs nor insufficiently deep ANNs with ReLU activation but can be approximated without the curse of dimensionality by sufficiently deep ANNs with ReLU activation. More specifically, we prove that in the case of shallow ReLU ANNs or deep ReLU ANNs with a fixed number of hidden layers we have that the number of ANN parameters must grow at least exponentially in the dimension $d\in\mathbb{N}$ while in the case of deep ReLU ANNs with the number of hidden layers growing in the dimension $d\in\mathbb{N}$ we have that the number of scalar real parameters of the approximating ANNs grows at most polynomially in both the inverse of the prescribed approximation accuracy $\varepsilon>0$ and the dimension $d\in\mathbb{N}$, even if the absolute values of the ANN parameters are assumed to be uniformly bounded by $1$.

replace-cross Randomized Forward Mode of Automatic Differentiation For Optimization Algorithms

Authors: Khemraj Shukla, Yeonjong Shin

Abstract: Reverse mode automatic differentiation (AD) computes a gradient in a single sweep, but at a memory cost that grows with the length of the computational graph. That cost becomes the binding constraint when the graph is long, as in parameter identification governed by differential equations or in training on memory-limited hardware. We study a randomized forward mode gradient (RFG) as an alternative to backpropagation in precisely this regime. RFG is a random gradient estimator built from the directional derivative along a random vector, a quantity that forward mode AD computes without storing a tape. Replacing the gradient with RFG yields a class of optimization algorithms; for RFG-based gradient descent (GD) and Polyak's heavy ball (PHB) methods, we establish convergence results on quadratic functions. The analysis reveals that using random vectors from a probability distribution with the smallest kurtosis yields the fastest rate of convergence. Computational experiments demonstrate the performance of the proposed algorithms and verify the theoretical findings, including beyond quadratic functions. We further characterize when RFG is computationally preferable to backpropagation. Since the variance of RFG grows with the parameter dimension $d$ while the memory required by the reverse mode grows with the length $\ell$ of the computational graph, the relevant figure of merit is the ratio $\ell/d$. We confirm this by measurement against reverse mode with checkpointing. For a physics-informed neural network, where $d$ is large and $\ell$ short, RFG is not competitive. For parameter identification governed by differential equations, where a handful of coefficients is recovered from a long time integration, RFG requires one to three orders of magnitude less memory. The price is a larger iteration count, which leaves its time to solution comparable to or worse than a well-tuned reverse mode.

replace-cross MMGenBench: An Automated Pipeline for Evaluating LMMs from a Text-to-Image Generation Perspective

Authors: Hailang Huang, Yong Wang, Zixuan Huang, Huaqiu Li, Tongwen Huang, Xiangxiang Chu

Abstract: Existing benchmarks often rely on predefined questions or reference captions, limiting their flexibility for evaluating detailed descriptions on new image collections. We present MMGenBench, an automated benchmark framework for evaluating Large Multimodal Models (LMMs) from raw images without per-image questions or reference captions. The pipeline generates descriptions, reconstructs images with a frozen text-to-image model, and compares the original and reconstructed images using a frozen image encoder. It produces reusable per-image scores and inspectable reconstructions, supporting both model comparison and diagnosis of reconstructible visual information. We construct MMGenBench-Domain with 10,000 generated images and MMGenBench-Test with 1,284 images spanning 13 overlapping visual patterns. Evaluation of 68 LMMs reveals pattern-specific differences in reconstruction quality that overall scores can obscure. On 1,000 human-labeled caption pairs from six models, the default score achieves 90.00\% sample-level agreement. Factual perturbations and component analyses characterize the score's sensitivity under the evaluated pipeline, while a COCO study provides initial validation on natural photographs. MMGenBench complements existing benchmarks by linking automated comparisons to visual evidence of the information preserved in LMM descriptions. Project page: https://github.com/lerogo/MMGenBench.

URLs: https://github.com/lerogo/MMGenBench.

replace-cross On the Efficiency of Structured Pruning in Small Language Model Pretraining

Authors: Yixiao Li, Xianzhi Du, Ajay Jaiswal, Tao Lei, Tuo Zhao, Chong Wang, Jianyu Wang

Abstract: Companies that deploy generative language models on edge devices often need to train small models from scratch due to legal, data-governance, or product-customization requirements. An alternative approach is to train an enlarged model and compress it to the target size via structured pruning, but this advantage becomes unclear once the cost of pretraining the enlarged model is included. We introduce the enlarge-and-prune framework to study this question under full-cost accounting, and show that conventional enlarge-and-prune pipelines -- which train the enlarged model, prune it, and recover in separate stages -- do not consistently outperform direct small-model training under the same total token budget. To overcome this limitation, we propose IDEA Prune, an integrated enlarge-and-prune pipeline that unifies enlarged model pretraining, iterative structured pruning, and recovery under a single cosine annealing learning rate schedule. In controlled experiments compressing 2.8B models to 1.3B with up to 2T tokens on the same dataset, IDEA Prune consistently outperforms conventional pipelines and modestly improves overall performance relative to the scratch baseline, improving MMLU accuracy to 46.4%, compared with 31.4-33.4% for conventional pipelines. These advantages are preserved after downstream supervised fine-tuning, where IDEA Prune matches or outperforms all baselines of the same size on instruction following, math reasoning, and code generation. Our analysis further reveals that intermediate checkpoints provide more favorable pruning starting points than fully converged ones, and that an enlargement factor of approximately 2.6x yields the best pruned models in the tested regime.

replace-cross Mamba-3D as Masked Autoencoders for Accurate and Data-Efficient Analysis of Medical Ultrasound Videos

Authors: Jiaheng Zhou, Yanfeng Zhou, Wei Fang, Yuxing Tang, Le Lu, Ge Yang

Abstract: Ultrasound videos are an important form of clinical imaging data, and deep learning-based automated analysis can improve diagnostic accuracy and clinical efficiency. replace-cross Causal pieces: analysing and improving spiking neural networks piece by piece

Authors: Dominik Dold, Philipp Christian Petersen

Abstract: We introduce "causal pieces", a novel concept for analysing spiking neural networks (SNNs), inspired by "linear pieces" used to study expressivity and trainability in artificial neural networks (ANNs). Causal pieces partition the input and parameter space of a feedforward SNN with single-spike coding into distinct regions where the same subnetwork causes the output spikes. For networks of current-based leaky integrate-and-fire (LIF) neurons with large membrane time constants, we show that within each causal piece, output spike times are locally Lipschitz continuous with respect to inputs and network parameters. We further prove a lower bound on the approximation error that depends on the number of causal pieces. Thus, the number of causal pieces is a measure of the approximation capabilities of SNNs, which is valid despite spike-time discontinuities and applies to networks with both excitatory and inhibitory synapses. Empirically, we find that parameter initialisations yielding more causal pieces on the training set strongly correlate with SNN training success across multiple benchmarks, including Yin-Yang, Fashion-MNIST, and EuroSAT. Moreover, simulations with standard single-spike LIF neurons indicate that our findings extend beyond the theoretically analysed regime. These results establish causal pieces as a powerful and principled tool for analysing and improving the computational capabilities of SNNs.

replace-cross VFRTok: Variable Frame Rates Video Tokenizer with Duration-Proportional Information Assumption

Authors: Tianxiong Zhong, Xingye Tian, Boyuan Jiang, Xuebo Wang, Xin Tao, Pengfei Wan, Zhiwei Zhang

Abstract: Modern video generation frameworks based on Latent Diffusion Models suffer from inefficiencies in tokenization due to the Frame-Proportional Information Assumption. Existing tokenizers provide fixed temporal compression rates, causing the computational cost of the diffusion model to scale linearly with the frame rate. The paper proposes the Duration-Proportional Information Assumption: the upper bound on the information capacity of a video is proportional to the duration rather than the number of frames. Based on this insight, the paper introduces VFRTok, a Transformer-based video tokenizer, that enables variable frame rate encoding and decoding through asymmetric frame rate training between the encoder and decoder. Furthermore, the paper proposes Partial Rotary Position Embeddings (RoPE) to decouple position and content modeling, which groups correlated patches into unified tokens. The Partial RoPE effectively improves content-awareness, enhancing the video generation capability. Benefiting from the compact and continuous spatio-temporal representation, VFRTok achieves competitive reconstruction quality and state-of-the-art generation fidelity while using only 1/8 tokens compared to existing tokenizers. The code and weights are released at: https://github.com/KwaiVGI/VFRTok.

URLs: https://github.com/KwaiVGI/VFRTok.

replace-cross TRIM: Achieving Extreme Sparsity with Targeted Row-wise Iterative Metric-driven Pruning

Authors: Florentin Beck, William Rudman, Carsten Eickhoff

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) present substantial computational and memory challenges. Existing one-shot pruning methods often use a uniform sparsity target for all rows within a layer, which can be too coarse at high sparsity. This work introduces TRIM (Targeted Row-wise Iterative Metric-driven pruning), a forward-statistics-based wrapper that allocates different sparsity ratios to individual output rows while keeping model weights frozen. TRIM separates the choice of which weights to prune (scoring) from how many to prune in each row (allocation), and composes with existing scorers and layer-wise allocations. Across Qwen2.5, LLaMA-2, and OPT models, its largest gains occur at 70-80% sparsity. At 80%, adding TRIM to OWL reduces WikiText-2 perplexity from 348.48 to 180.67 for Qwen2.5-14B and from 6461.43 to 324.14 for OPT-13B. We conclude that fine-grained, row-wise allocation is an effective mechanism for substantially improving one-shot pruning quality in the extreme-sparsity regime. Code: https://github.com/flobk/TRIM.

URLs: https://github.com/flobk/TRIM.

replace-cross AIR: Rethinking Image-Text Offset Alignment in Multimodal Contrastive Representation Space

Authors: Guimeng Liu, Milad Abdollahzadeh, Ngai-Man Cheung

Abstract: Image-text offset alignment, which refers to the consistency between visual transformation directions and their corresponding textual transformation directions, has become an important relational property in multimodal contrastive representation spaces such as CLIP and SigLIP. This property plays a central role in text-driven visual transformation tasks, including generative model domain adaptation and text-guided object-centric image editing, where textual offsets are used to guide visual transformations. These methods fundamentally rely on the assumption that image and text offsets are well aligned, such that textual transformation directions can provide reliable guidance for corresponding visual transformations. However, the validity of this alignment assumption has not been systematically examined. In this work, we question this foundational assumption by conducting a comprehensive empirical analysis of image-text offset alignment in multimodal contrastive representation space. Our findings reveal not only noticeable offset misalignment but also a meaningful positive correlation between image-text offset misalignment and semantic concept distance across six large datasets and eight contrastive vision-language models. Based on this discovery, we propose Adaptation with Iterative Refinement (AIR), a method that iteratively refines text offsets through anchor sampling and our proposed concept description learning to mitigate the downstream effect of image-text offset misalignment and improve guidance accuracy. Comprehensive experiments on zero-shot generative model domain adaptation and text-guided object-centric image editing across 40 setups, including qualitative, quantitative, and user studies, consistently show that AIR enables state-of-the-art performance in these tasks.

replace-cross Sample-Aware Test-Time Adaptation for Medical Image-to-Image Translation

Authors: Irene Iele, Francesco Di Feola, Matteo Tortora, Rosa Sicilia, Valerio Guarrasi, Paolo Soda

Abstract: Image-to-image translation has emerged as a powerful technique in medical imaging, enabling tasks such as image denoising and cross-modality conversion. To address this limitation, we propose a novel Test-Time Adaptation (TTA) framework that dynamically adjusts the translation process based on the characteristics of each test sample. Our method introduces a Reconstruction Module to quantify the domain shift and a Dynamic Adaptation Block that selectively modifies the internal features of a pretrained translation model to mitigate the shift without compromising the performance on in-distribution samples that do not require adaptation. The code is available at: https://github.com/Sample-Aware-TTA/Code.

replace-cross One-Shot Clustering for Federated Learning Under Clustering-Agnostic Assumption

Authors: Maciej Krzysztof Zuziak, Roberto Pellungrini, Salvatore Rinzivillo

Abstract: Federated Learning (FL) is a widespread and well-adopted paradigm of decentralised learning that allows training one model from multiple sources without the need to transfer data between participating clients directly. Since its inception in 2016, it has been divided into numerous subfields that deal with application-specific issues, such as data heterogeneity or resource allocation. One such sub-field, Clustered Federated Learning (CFL), is dealing with the problem of clustering the population of clients into separate cohorts to deliver personalised models. Although a few remarkable works have been published in this domain, the problem remains largely unexplored, as its basic assumptions and settings differ slightly from those of standard FL. We also revisit the practical feasibility of CFL algorithms based on the gradients or weights of the clients, providing firm evidence of the high efficiency of density-based clustering methods when used to differentiate between the loss surfaces of neural networks trained on different distributions. Moreover, by inspecting the feasibility of local explanations generated with the help of GradCAM, we can provide more insights into the relationship between personalisation and the explainability of local predictions.

replace-cross Complexity-Regularized Proximal Policy Optimization

Authors: Luca Serfilippi, Giorgio Franceschelli, Antonio Corradi, Mirco Musolesi

Abstract: Policy gradient methods usually rely on entropy regularization to prevent premature convergence. However, maximizing entropy indiscriminately pushes the policy towards a uniform distribution, often overriding the reward signal if not optimally tuned. We propose replacing the standard entropy term with a self-regulating complexity term, defined as the product of Shannon entropy and disequilibrium, which quantifies the distance from the uniform distribution. Unlike pure entropy, which favors maximal disorder, this complexity measure is zero for both fully deterministic and perfectly uniform distributions, i.e., it is strictly positive for systems that exhibit a meaningful interplay between order and randomness. These properties ensure the policy maintains beneficial stochasticity while reducing regularization pressure when the policy is highly uncertain, allowing learning to focus on reward optimization. We introduce Complexity-Regularized Proximal Policy Optimization (CR-PPO), a modification of PPO that leverages this dynamic. We empirically demonstrate that CR-PPO is significantly more robust to hyperparameter selection than entropy-regularized PPO, achieving consistent performance across orders of magnitude of regularization coefficients and remaining harmless when regularization is unnecessary, thereby reducing the need for expensive hyperparameter tuning.

replace-cross Rethinking Multiplication in Neural Networks with Hadamard Transform-Assisted Operators

Authors: Emadeldeen Hamdan, Marouane Tliba, Ulas Bagci, Ahmet Enis Cetin

Abstract: Multiplication-avoiding neural operators are attractive for compute-in-memory (CIM) architectures, but directly replacing conventional convolutions often causes accuracy degradation. We investigate whether this limitation arises from multiplication avoidance itself or from the representation on which the operator acts. In this work, we introduce Hadamard Transform-assisted multiplication-avoiding (HMA) operators that combine Walsh--Hadamard transform features with multiplication-avoiding channel mixing. To identify where conventional multiplication remains necessary, we employ a gated formulation in which conventional and HMA operators compete through learned layer-wise coefficients. ResNet-18 experiments show that intermediate convolutional layers assign greater weight to HMA, whereas the earliest layers and final classifier retain greater contribution from conventional computation. Guided by this observation, we construct a compact hybrid HMA network and train it using knowledge distillation from a conventional teacher. On CIFAR-10, the resulting ResNet-18 student achieves 91.2\% accuracy compared with 90.4\% for the corresponding conventional baseline while reducing parameters by 68.25\%, conventional MACs by 85.71\%, and general multiplications by 85.54\%. Across ResNet-18, ResNet-50, and MobileNetV2 on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and Tiny-ImageNet, HMA with knowledge distillation matches or improves the conventional models. These results show that Hadamard-domain feature restructuring can improve the accuracy--complexity tradeoff of multiplication-avoiding networks and provide a practical path toward efficient CIM inference.

replace-cross Binding Constraints in a Strategic Technology: An Industrial Vulnerability Index for the Foundation Model Industry

Authors: Claudio Pirrone, Stefano Fricano, Gioacchino Fazio

Abstract: Industrial policy directed at artificial intelligence has become substantial. Public investment concentrates on compute, where private capacity expansion is already underway, and is largely absent from grid interconnection and generation. Whether that allocation accurately reflects the most binding constraint in production has not yet been systematically measured. This paper introduces the Artificial Intelligence Industrial Vulnerability Index (AIIVI) as a framework for assessing potential production constraints in frontier AI. We construct a composite index of input vulnerability for the foundation model industry from fourteen concentration and sustainability parameters, aggregated by an O-Ring functional form in which capability is strongly influenced by the most constrained input. AIIVI should not be interpreted as a direct measurement of production constraints. Rather, it provides an index-based approximation derived from observable indicators and weighting assumptions designed to capture relative exposure to input vulnerabilities. Using this transparent and reproducible framework for estimating the relative vulnerability of frontier AI production to disruptions across key input dimensions, we also evaluate the index at 31 August 2026 for the nine developers at the capability frontier. We find values of 0.75 under AI-elicited weights and 0.62 under equal weights. The analysis identifies compute and energy as the principal drivers of input vulnerability. Marginal changes in these dimensions produce substantially larger variations in AIIVI than comparable changes in capital, talent, or data. We highlight three exposures that receive limited attention in existing assessment frameworks: the increasing reliance on external debt for data-centre financing, local opposition as a constraint on data-centre siting, and export controls targeting deployed AI models rather than hardware.

replace-cross Taming Hallucinations: Boosting MLLMs' Video Understanding via Counterfactual Video Generation

Authors: Zhe Huang, Hao Wen, Aiming Hao, Bingze Song, Meiqi Wu, Jiahong Wu, Xiangxiang Chu, Sheng Lu, Haoqian Wang

Abstract: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have made remarkable progress in video understanding. However, they suffer from a critical vulnerability: an over-reliance on language priors, which can lead to visual ungrounded hallucinations, especially when processing counterfactual videos that defy common sense. This limitation, stemming from the intrinsic data imbalance between text and video, is challenging to address due to the substantial cost of collecting and annotating counterfactual data. To address this, we introduce DualityForge, a novel counterfactual data synthesis framework that employs controllable, diffusion-based video editing to transform real-world videos into counterfactual scenarios. By embedding structured contextual information into the video editing and QA generation processes, the framework automatically produces high-quality QA pairs together with original-edited video pairs for contrastive training. Based on this, we build DualityVidQA, a large-scale video dataset designed to reduce MLLM hallucinations. In addition, to fully exploit the contrastive nature of our paired data, we propose Duality-Normalized Advantage Training (DNA-Train), a two-stage SFT-RL training regime where the RL phase applies pair-wise $\ell_1$ advantage normalization, thereby enabling a more stable and efficient policy optimization. Experiments on DualityVidQA-Test demonstrate that our method substantially reduces model hallucinations on counterfactual videos, yielding a relative improvement of 24.0% over the Qwen2.5-VL-7B baseline. Moreover, our approach achieves significant gains across both hallucination and general-purpose benchmarks, indicating strong generalization capability. We will open-source our dataset and code.

replace-cross Multi-Dimensional Prompt Chaining to Improve Open-Domain Dialogue Generation

Authors: Livia Leong Hui Teng, Jing Yang Lee

Abstract: Small language models (SLMs) offer significant deployment advantages but often struggle to match the dialogue quality of larger models in open-domain settings. In this paper, we propose a multi-dimensional prompt-chaining framework that integrates Naturalness, Coherence, and Engagingness dimensions to enhance human-likeness in open-domain dialogue generation. We apply the framework to two SLMs, TinyLlama and Llama-2-7B, and benchmark their performance against responses generated by substantially larger models, including Llama-2-70B and GPT-3.5 Turbo. We then employ automatic and human evaluation to assess the responses based on diversity, contextual coherence, as well as overall quality. Results show that the full framework improves response diversity by up to 29%, contextual coherence by up to 28%, and engagingness as well as naturalness by up to 29%. Notably, Llama-2-7B achieves performance comparable to substantially larger models, including Llama-2-70B and GPT-3.5 Turbo. Overall, the findings demonstrate that carefully designed prompt-based strategies provide an effective and resource-efficient pathway to improving open-domain dialogue quality in SLMs.

Abstract: Foundation models are used for many real-world applications involving language understanding and generation from temporally-ordered multimodal events. replace-cross Linguistic and Argument Diversity in Synthetic Data for Function-Calling Agents

Authors: Dan Greenstein, Zohar Karnin, Chen Amiraz, Oren Somekh

Abstract: The construction of function calling agents has emerged as a promising avenue for extending model capabilities.
