replace-cross FlashReFocus: Interactive Image Refocusing in Seconds

Authors: Ching-Heng Cheng, Chia-Ming Lee, Ming-Ching Chang, Xin Li, Chih-Chung Hsu

Abstract: Single-image refocusing changes the focal plane and depth of field of a photograph after capture. Users compare focal choices on one photograph, so content stability across edits matters alongside quality and speed. Recent generative methods rerun large diffusion models for every edit, even when they first restore an all-in-focus image, so content can change inconsistently between edits. Yet only this restoration is highly ill-posed and benefits from a generative prior, whereas rendering blur is largely determined by depth and the focal plane. We therefore propose FlashReFocus, which runs diffusion once per photograph and renders each edit with a lightweight network. A one-step diffusion deblurrer with a decoder refiner recovers fine detail efficiently while balancing fidelity and perceptual quality. The renderer uses an explicit defocus map for control and defocus-bias attention to promote information exchange between tokens with similar blur magnitudes. Because every edit reuses the same reconstruction, in-focus content stays consistent across edits, and editing the map enables tilt-shift and two-plane effects. We further collect 3CReal, a benchmark of 89 pairs from three camera-lens systems, to evaluate generalization to unseen optics. For zero-shot bokeh rendering on EBB! Val176 and 3CReal, FlashReFocus leads the evaluated methods in fidelity and perceptual quality. At 1536x1536, a complete refocus takes 2.45 s and each subsequent edit 0.46 s on one RTX PRO 6000.

replace-cross Red-Teaming Multi-Agent Attacks for Loss-of-Control Risks

Authors: John Kitaoka, Thao Amelia Pham, Carlos Rafael Giudice, Jacob Merizian

Abstract: AI agents are growing more capable and persistent, and are being deployed in ever larger numbers. As more start collaborating, AI control measures must remain effective when they covertly collude on a harmful objective. We introduce MiniLab, a synthetic series of live services and codebases (5 interconnected services, 249 benign tasks, 8 attack objectives), adapted from internal AI deployment infrastructure. We demonstrate distributed attacks, in which different agents carry out separate attack fragments, and how their covertness evades frontier monitors when reading their trajectories individually. We show how distributed attacks spread across diverse contexts and harmful objectives through 16 human-optimized attacks (weight exfiltration, unmonitored rogue deployment, permission elevation, and more). A frontier monitor, GPT-5.6 Sol, reading each agent separately flags only 3.4% of successful distributed attacks, compared with 82.4% when a single agent carries out the same side task. Individual attacks can also be composed into longer attack sequences toward a greater loss-of-control outcome, without any individual agent being flagged. Finally, we tested whether models can plan these covert attacks: even with execution and monitor feedback, the best model-planned attacks succeed covertly under both their feedback monitor and GPT-5.6 Sol in at most 32% of executions, compared with 82% for human-crafted plans, though this may scale with model capabilities. By turning worst-case coordinated attacks into reproducible red-team cases, our work provides a foundation for preventing locally benign agent actions from accumulating into system-level loss of control.

replace-cross One Evaluation Is Enough: Inversion-Free Image Editing with Trajectory Correction

Authors: Lifan Jiang, Taiyang Chen, Senlin cheng, Bolei Xu, Xiaobo Li, Can Ye, Dehai Zhao, Deng Cai, Boxi Wu

Abstract: Real-image editing commonly relies on inversion and iterative sampling, while efficient one-step editors often stabilize a large update by estimating and averaging editing fields at multiple noise levels. We ask a stricter question: is a single temporal field evaluation sufficient for reliable inversion-free editing? Our answer rests on a previously underused duality in a variance-preserving (VP) one-step model: one source-target query at a shared noisy state simultaneously determines a local angular semantic tangent and a clean endpoint displacement. We integrate the tangent over the analytically known remaining VP angle to obtain a nominal one-step transport, and use the endpoint displacement to correct its long-horizon extrapolation through a constrained projection. This transport-first, correction-second construction yields Endpoint-Calibrated Transport (ECT), a closed-form, training-free editor requiring only one noise level, two text conditions packed into one denoiser batch, and one latent update. ECT requires no image inversion, trajectory integration, second temporal observation, or iterative optimization, and can be directly applied to pretrained epsilon-prediction VP one-step generators. Across the reported benchmark settings, ECT delivers stable gains in editing quality within an extremely short editing time.

replace-cross A Mathematical Theory of the Jacobian Lens

Authors: Mitchell Ostrow, Daoyuan Qian, Toni Liu, Ila Fiete

Abstract: Recent work from Anthropic introduced a new interpretability tool, called the Jacobian lens (J-lens, Gurnee et al. 2026). This method identified linear directions in the hidden states of frontier language models that had properties reminiscent of working-memory-like representations. However, it is unclear what mechanistic properties of such features make them detectable by the J-lens. To this end, we introduce a mathematical theory of the J-lens and show that it preferentially identifies coherent features--directions that act like leading, spatially coherent finite-time Lyapunov modes. Small perturbations along these directions maximally amplify across layers, remain roughly constant across token positions, and are consistent across different contexts. While we are agnostic to whether these properties are necessarily associated with a "Global Workspace'', the analytically derived preferences of the J-lens nevertheless explain prior empirical results. We validate our analysis on a Bayes filter and a toy transformer, each optimally predicting next tokens emitted from a Hidden Markov Model (HMM). We show how modulating task parameters that change the coherence of features in the Bayes filter correspondingly and predictively modulates the response of the J-lens applied to both model classes. We also introduce a family of J-lenses built to emphasize different temporal scales of information processing in LLMs, which we call $\omega$-J-lenses.

replace-cross EvolveMAS: A Unified Self-Evolution Framework for Decentralized Multi-Agent Systems

Authors: Yalun Wu, Qiwei Ma, Bingbing Xu, Dan Zhang, Long Bai, Boyang Wang, Jiatong Li, Zheqi Lv, Shaozu Yuan, Peiying Wang, Jiawei Wang, An Zhang, Tat-Seng Chua

Abstract: Decentralized multi-agent systems (MAS) enable large language model agents to interact without centralized control. Agents need to adapt to other agents at test time and reuse accumulated experience to refine their interaction strategies for future interactions across cooperative, mixed-motive, and adversarial settings. However, existing approaches often develop adaptation mechanisms for particular interaction settings. This raises a central question: how can decentralized MAS support test-time adaptation and reliable experience reuse across interaction settings? We propose EvolveMAS, a unified self-evolution framework that supports experience-driven adaptation within and across episodes. EvolveMAS pairs each main agent with a dedicated MemoryAgent for test-time memory governance, which maintains isolated Structured Memory outside the main agent's reasoning context. EvolveMAS organizes adaptation into two complementary processes: Within-Episode Online Adaptation and Cross-Episode Knowledge Evolution. During Within-Episode Online Adaptation, MemoryAgent updates episodic, semantic, and working memory from interaction evidence to inform subsequent actions. During Cross-Episode Knowledge Evolution, the main agent uses memory and episode outcomes to distill reusable interaction knowledge comprising partner profiles and interaction strategies for subsequent episodes. Across five benchmarks and six evaluation configurations spanning the three interaction settings, EvolveMAS achieves the highest normalized average score of 77.2 among the compared methods. Ablations show that test-time memory governance and cross-episode knowledge reuse both improve performance. Interaction traces further illustrate how updated memory and distilled knowledge guide subsequent interactions. These results support experience-driven adaptation in decentralized MAS without updating model parameters.

replace-cross Ranking Function Generation with LLMs for Termination

Authors: Rongkang Li, Jiaxin Hu, Taolue Chen, Yong Li, Fu Song, Zhilin Wu, Qiusong Yang

Abstract: Ranking functions are a fundamental technique for proving loop termination, but synthesizing them remains challenging and often requires substantial expertise. Despite considerable progress in their automated synthesis, existing approaches remain limited in their effectiveness. We propose RAINFALL, a neuro-symbolic framework that tightly integrates large language models (LLMs) with symbolic verification. To our best knowledge, RAINFALL is the first to employ a generate-verify-refine paradigm for LLM-based ranking function synthesis. RAINFALL formulates ranking function synthesis as a constrained code-completion task, inserting placeholders into the input program that the LLM must fill in four predefined slots per loop. The resulting program is checked by a symbolic verifier. Successful verification yields a valid ranking function, while verification failures produce diagnostics that are translated into natural-language feedback to guide iterative refinement. We evaluate RAINFALL on two public benchmark suites comprising 358 terminating C programs. Using DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash, RAINFALL synthesizes verified ranking functions for 333 programs (93.02%), outperforming all evaluated baselines. Notably, it solves 22 more programs than PROTON 2.1, the winner of the SV-COMP 2026 Termination Category. Combining RAINFALL with PROTON 2.1 further increases the success rate to 96.65% (346/358 programs). Ablation studies demonstrate the contributions of placeholder contracts, few-shot examples, symbolic verification, and diagnostic feedback, while experiments across different LLMs establish the broader applicability of the framework.

replace-cross ResCom: Activation Residual Compensation for Large Language Model Quantization

Authors: Ning Yang, Bohan Cui, Zhuowen Liu, Yuting Wang

Abstract: Low-bit weight--activation quantization reduces the memory and computation needed to deploy large language models, but activation rounding can still degrade accuracy after calibration. Reconstruction from rounded inputs cannot distinguish activations within the same quantization bin. We propose ResCom, a low-rank correction that uses the activation rounding residual to compensate for the resulting layer-output error. An output-aware calibration procedure selects residual directions by their contribution to output reconstruction and computes the correction in closed form, without gradient training or updates to the calibrated weights. On Llama-3-8B with 4-bit weights and activations, rank-32 attention corrections reduce WikiText-2 perplexity from 7.708 to 7.147, improve MMLU by 2.89 percentage points, and raise five-shot GSM8K accuracy from 23.96 to 36.16 percent. Evaluations on models from 1B to 70B parameters and on held-out C4 test the method beyond this setting. The evaluated FP32 implementation adds 2.25 percent to 4K prefill time; a separate compact-storage implementation retains a 27.7 percent peak-memory reduction relative to FP16.

replace-cross Atom as an Agent: Emergent Order by Collective Predictive Coding

Authors: Koji Hashimoto, Tadahiro Taniguchi

Abstract: What is the intrinsic reason of emergence of macroscopic order parameter out of microscopic systems? In this article, we apply to physics the collective predictive coding (CPC), the theory of language/symbol emergence. As our language emerges out of our human communications, physical macroscopic order parameter emerges out of communications among atoms --- each atom is regarded as an agent who predicts each other's behavior through inference. We present three examples of classical spin systems: two-dimensional ferromagnetic and antiferromagnetic Ising models, a two-dimensional spin model with competing stripe orders, and the fully connected Sherrington--Kirkpatrick spin-glass model. In all of these examples, proper macroscopic order parameters including the replica overlap emerge automatically through communication informatics of CPC, only from observed spin configurations. We call the learned macroscopic coordinate the collective predictive order. Furthermore, for the ferromagnetic and antiferromagnetic Ising models, the equilibrium distribution of the collective predictive order yields an emergent potential with the symmetry and thermal evolution characteristic of a Landau potential. Notably, the emergence of the collective predictive order does not refer to any structure of microscopic Hamiltonians/Lagrangians. Thus our ``Atom-as-an-Agent" (AaaA) principle offers a completely data-driven method for the emergence of macroscopic order, that is, machine learning physics.

Authors: Patrick Knab, David Steinmann, Giovanni De Felice, Felix Divo, Udo Schlegel, Wolfgang Stammer, Sascha Marton, Christian Bartelt

replace-cross Demystifying Prompt Stealing Attacks against Synthetic Video

Authors: Junyan Huang, Sanskar Agmain, Chen Gong, Yan Pang, FNU Suya

Abstract: Creators publicly showcase generated content to advertise and sell their work, but these examples may also enable unauthorized reproduction and customization. We study whether an adversary can derive reusable inputs from a single public video for faithful reconstruction and controlled editing, without access to the original prompt or generator. We adapt captioning and prompt optimization approaches to video prompt stealing attacks (PSAs) and find that prompt recovery alone provides limited reconstruction fidelity. Even the original prompt produces different spatial and temporal realizations when the initial noise changes. Using an independent generator enlarges the reconstruction gap, as reusing the original prompt and seed can still produce substantially different videos. We propose V-PSA, which uses a recovered caption and DDIM inversion to construct a reference trajectory under an independent generator. Null-Text Inversion then optimizes per-step unconditional embeddings to reduce deviations from this trajectory during reconstruction. We construct a benchmark containing 12,000 video-prompt pairs from three victim generators and four prompt sources. V-PSA achieves video-to-video similarity around 0.9 between target and reconstructed videos, substantially improving over prompt-only baselines with similarity score of 0.6, and supports multiple controlled variations that preserve the target's structure. Additional experiments show that augmenting prior image PSAs with inversion-derived inputs also improves reconstruction. Our results show that evaluations restricted to prompt recovery can underestimate an attacker's ability to reconstruct public generative outputs.

replace-cross Sparse Autoencoder Attribution Graphs Are Decided at the Margin

Authors: Karl Li

Abstract: Interpretability research writes a language model's activation as a weighted sum of learned directions, called features. A sparse autoencoder (SAE) learns the features and gives each a coefficient, its weight in that sum. The SAE's encoder first gives every feature a score. A TopK SAE selects the k features with the largest positive scores and zeroes the rest. A feature's margin is how far its score must move before the selection changes. An intervention on one feature's coefficient changes the encoder scores of the SAE at the next layer. An attribution graph predicts that change from the scores' derivatives with respect to the coefficient, taken before the intervention. The graph keeps the original selection, so it misses every feature that enters or leaves. We propose the margin graph, which adds the predicted change to every score and selects again. When one feature is zeroed in a public pair of Pythia-70M SAEs, the median error of the predicted coefficient change, relative to the true change, falls from 0.739 for the attribution graph to 0.121 for the margin graph. Written back into the model, the margin graph's coefficients predict the change in the model's output on subject verb agreement less accurately than the attribution graph's. Selecting again better predicts which features change, but on the task and pair we tested, not the model's output.

replace-cross Lightweight Representation Learning for Low Tucker Rank Tensors

Authors: Hiroki Hasegawa, Yukihiko Okada

Abstract: Iterative estimators for low Tucker rank tensors, such as HOOI, nuclear-norm proximal and ADMM methods, Frank-Wolfe and scaled gradient descent, form large objects in every iteration: unfoldings, Kronecker operators, full SVDs and ambient iterates. The update uses little of these objects, yet they make memory the limiting resource on large tensors and on edge-sized devices. Remedies for each of these costs are known. However, it is unclear when a rewriting keeps the iterates unchanged, and where it pays off against efficient implementations. We therefore present a trajectory-preserving lightweight evaluation. First, we state exactness as an intertwining relation between the stored state and the reference iteration. Second, we show that three known components satisfy this relation for five estimators: computing only the singular triplets that an update needs, contracting without Kronecker products, and storing sparse-plus-factored states. As a result, the iterates and all guarantees of the reference estimator stay the same. On an A100 GPU, with the input data counted in the memory, lightweight HOOI needs the least memory of 15 HOOI-type methods at every size ($95\times$ less beyond the input than the lightest exact implementation on $800^3$ tensors), and it is the fastest method that reaches the HOOI estimate on the largest tensors. Against efficient public code, ours is faster and lighter for three of five estimators. On a surveillance video and a satellite hyperspectral cube, ours is the only implementation that fits a 1 GiB and a 4 GiB device. Finally, we show that the memory and time gains need separate conditions, and we identify where these conditions fail.

replace-cross dKV Forcing: Separating Generation and Memory Representations through a Differentiable KV Cache

Authors: Zhiqi Li, Bo Zhu

Abstract: A causal video generator writes every segment it produces into a key--value (KV) cache, and later segments know the past only through it. Self-rollout training stops the gradient at this cache, and Self Gradient Forcing (SGF) restores it, but applies it to the same weights that generate the current segment. We find that writing and generating are different roles: in a pretrained generator the written KV already differs from the KV used for denoising, and on shared weights the gradient from future reads is negatively aligned with the gradient of generation. We propose dKV Forcing (dKV denotes differentiable KV), which gives the write its own parameters, a low-rank residual used only when a segment enters the cache, and trains them with the gradient of the later segments that read what they wrote. Opening this gradient through the cache in common use exhausts GPU memory. We trace this to checkpoint replay re-writing the cache, not to the gradient path, which removes the need for the second reconstruction pass of SGF. dKV Forcing keeps the cache as persistent differentiable pages and computes the exact gradient in one temporal traversal, with an $11\%$ lower memory peak and $28\%$ less resident memory. With pages that store hidden states, it trains 15\,s clips on which the two-pass reconstruction runs out of memory. Trained on 5\,s clips, dKV Forcing reaches the highest VBench-Long quality score at 60\,s and 240\,s and outperforms SGF by $3.6$ and $6.1$ points of aesthetic quality, with a higher dynamic degree.

replace-cross On Agentic Manipulation Policies

Authors: Omid Bagherieh, Naveen Nikamanth Appiah Balaji, Chen Chen, Jesse T. Gonzalez, Negin Heravi, Fahad Islam, Xueting Li, Jean-Baptiste Regli, Kejia Ren, Petr \v{S}vec, David J. Yoon, Matthias M\"uller, Vladlen Koltun

Abstract: Lane-level maps provide foundational structured road information for navigation, intelligent driving, and intelligent transportation systems. replace-cross Speculative Self-Distillation enables Efficient Knowledge Internalization

Authors: Shayan Talaei, Agam Bhatia, Arshia Soltani Moakhar, Jonas H\"ubotter, Amin Saberi, Azalia Mirhoseini

Abstract: Many real-world applications require language models to internalize knowledge that was absent from pretraining, to incorporate continually updated facts, or to learn new domain skills. Self-distillation has emerged as a natural approach for this task. A model conditioned on the source material acts as the teacher, and an unconditioned student is trained to reproduce the teacher's behavior closed-book. We propose Speculative Self-Distillation (SSD), an efficient per-token mixed-policy method for self-distillation. The student generates each rollout by default, while the teacher intervenes only at positions where its next-token distribution substantially diverges from the student's. Unlike off-policy distillation with teacher-generated prefixes, this design keeps training rollouts close to the student's test-time behavior, while avoiding the low-signal states that fully on-policy rollouts drift into. In effect, SSD lets the teacher steer the rollout exactly where the privileged context changes the prediction. On three closed-book QA benchmarks measuring fact recall, knowledge updates, and skill acquisition, SSD nearly matches on-policy distillation while using 38-69% fewer supervised tokens and consistently outperforms off-policy distillation on the held-out test set. On a forgetting stress test, SSD retains the most general capability while also achieving the best in-domain accuracy and using less supervision than the on-policy baseline. Overall, SSD establishes a new accuracy-efficiency-forgetting Pareto frontier for self-distillation.

replace-cross From Attack Surface to Defense Architecture: Compositional Security for Agentic Coding Harnesses

Authors: Simon Hegelich, Habiba Sarhan, Morteza Shahrezaye, Kolja Hegelich, Christian Montag, Ralf Schneider, Erik Cambria

Abstract: AI coding harnesses couple language models to shells, filesystems, and network sockets so that agents can resolve issues and run tests. That coupling turns the harness into an automated Living-off-the-Land (LotL) platform: once an adversary can inject instructions into retrieved content, dual-use binaries such as bash, curl, and find become the payload rather than a vulnerability to be separately exploited. We argue that security in agentic systems is not an emergent behavioral property of individual language models, but a structural property of the cognitive architecture and of the composition of permissions across interacting agents and humans. Static regex blacklists miss flag permutations and structural attacks, whereas LLM-as-a-Judge guardrails ask a second model to guess physical invariants at tens to hundreds of milliseconds per call while over-blocking benign work. We present ActiveSecHarness, a neurosymbolic reference-monitor architecture organized around Separation of Intelligence and Authority across four pillars: (1) Macaroon-inspired HMAC capability tokens across four Trust and Authority Rings; (2) a product taint lattice L_trust x L_sens and a compositional Separation-of-Duties ledger over shared workspace provenance; (3) deterministic OS/subprocess guards; and (4) extended bytecode certificates, split-horizon dual-key audit logs, a self-healing verification loop (CodeVerifier, DualModelReviewer, SecurityTestSynthesizer), and a progressive pedagogical UserWikiManager. On N=252 fixtures and a 12+12 multi-step trajectory suite, Ring 0 achieves FPR=0.000 (0.939 unfitted out-of-sample / 0.962 OS-inert; 1.000 post-audit conformance ABR) in 31.5 us median latency (4,517x faster than a live local LLM-judge) and blocks 12/12 multi-step kill chains at 0.000 benign trajectory FPR (266.7 us/step; ~24 us/step O(1) ledger state folding).

replace-cross Visual Similarity in Adult Word Extension and Artificial Word Learning

Authors: Patricio M. Vera

Abstract: When two objects share a name, how much does their visual similarity explain, and what do the teaching examples add? We studied word extension across ten visual representations: eight trained encoders, a randomly initialized network, and raw pixels. Reanalyzing trial-level data from a large adult word-extension study (Lewis & Frank, 2018; 520 people), we computed each test image's distance to the teaching examples. Within the taught domain, greater distance was associated with less extension for all eight trained encoders and for the untrained network, so the association cannot be attributed only to what the encoders learned; raw pixels showed no negative association. In post hoc models that let the distance slope vary across images, seven of the eight trained encoders retained a negative estimate and five had 95% intervals entirely below zero; the interval of the reference encoder included zero. Whether trial order changes the distance slope, the frozen primary question, remained inconclusive. Example composition changed extension at comparable distances: after three examples, adults extended a word to 1.1% of superordinate items when the examples were subordinate and to 77.5% when they were superordinate. In artificial learners, words joining more distant categories were learned less accurately for six of the eight trained encoders. Every trained encoder correlated with human similarity measures from three datasets, and so did the untrained network, although far more weakly than the trained encoders other than MAE on two of them. In an exploratory comparison on the same images, no common threshold on any trained encoder reproduced the adults' narrow-to-broad gain (at most 66.67 vs. 76.43 percentage points). Cosine distance to the centroid of the teaching examples predicts part of word extension, but does not capture the effect of example composition.

replace-cross Editable Video Context: Mid-Context KV-Cache Reuse for Context-Managing Video LLMs

Authors: Dayu Wang, Shasha Xu

Abstract: Context Language Models let a model edit its own context, while Suffix Cache Reuse makes edits to text efficient by adjusting the positions of cached keys for surviving tokens. We extend these ideas to video language models with multimodal rotary position embeddings (M-RoPE). We represent a long video as timestamped segments. After viewing a sparsely sampled version, the model selects segments for decoding at 4x the frame rate, and the remaining segments are removed from its context. Our rebuild method reuses the KV cache of surviving segments by rotating their keys with a single offset shared by the temporal, height, and width components of Qwen2.5-VL's M-RoPE. A content-hashed cache also avoids repeating vision-encoder computation for those segments. In preliminary experiments with Qwen2.5-VL-7B on synthetic needle-in-a-video-haystack questions over public-domain films, the method reduces prefill tokens and end-to-end rebuild latency relative to full recomputation while usually producing the same answer. Densifying the segment containing the answer improves accuracy when that segment is known. When the model selects segments itself, accuracy does not improve: it rarely identifies a needle that was absent from the sparse sample. We release the code, raw logs, and scripts used to reproduce the paper's results.

replace-cross On-policy distillation as a Backdoor Propagation Channel

Authors: Zane Fu, Yanzhao Zheng, Ziting Qin, Qianshan Wei, Yi Yu, Gongli Xi, Jihuai Zhu, Baohua Dong, Hangcheng Zhu, Ruohui Huang, Gang Yu

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) is emerging as an important paradigm for the post training of large language models, improving student models by leveraging supervision generated online by teacher models. However, whether this process also transfers latent security risks from teacher models to student models remains insufficiently studied. In this work, we reveal a new security risk in which an attacker first implants a conditional backdoor into the teacher model through supervised fine tuning (SFT), after which OPD can transfer the backdoor to the student model with only an extremely small proportion of trigger prompt exposure. More concerningly, we find that such backdoor transfer is substantially stronger under OPD than under conventional distillation methods, suggesting that OPD may provide an efficient channel for propagating backdoors across models. Our mechanistic analysis shows that the backdoor signal is primarily transmitted through anomalous differences in the conditional probabilities produced by the teacher model for triggered and clean inputs, and is gradually internalized into the internal representations of the student model during training. Furthermore, we investigate how to suppress such anomalous conditional probability signals at the source of distillation supervision and, based on this analysis, propose an OPD training method tailored to this conditional backdoor propagation setting to mitigate the risk of backdoor transfer induced by untrusted teacher supervision.

replace-cross As Student Rollouts Continue, Visual Backdoors Keep Growing

Authors: Zane Fu, Yanzhao Zheng, Jinze Liu, Ziting Qin, Qianshan Wei, Jihuai Zhu, Yipeng Yu, Baohua Dong, Hangcheng Zhu, Ruohui Huang

Abstract: Conventional knowledge distillation can facilitate the propagation of backdoors from teacher models to student models. However, how this risk manifests in On-Policy Distillation (OPD) remains insufficiently studied. OPD has recently emerged as an important post-training paradigm for Vision-Language Models (VLMs). Unlike traditional offline distillation, OPD does not require teacher-generated responses. Instead, it uses the teacher's probability distributions to supervise response trajectories generated online by the student. Since the initial student model typically generates benign responses and receives no explicit supervision from malicious target responses, OPD appears to offer some inherent resistance to backdoor propagation. In this paper, we challenge this intuition and reveal a more subtle risk of visual backdoor propagation in OPD. Through experiments across multiple model families and datasets, together with theoretical analysis, we identify three main findings. First, unlike the relatively gradual propagation observed in traditional offline distillation, backdoors rarely emerge in OPD at low poisoning ratios. However, the attack success rate rapidly approaches 100% once a critical threshold is exceeded. Second, backdoor propagation does not depend on explicit malicious responses. Instead, it is driven by the continuous accumulation of anomalous probability mass assigned by the teacher. Finally, based on these findings, we propose a novel trigger design that enables visual backdoor propagation with zero explicit trigger injection. These findings further reveal an overlooked mechanism of backdoor propagation in OPD and highlight its potential security risks.

replace-cross Hypnos: A Dual-Phase End-to-End Platform for Scalable Continual Learning at the Edge

Authors: Guilherme Guedes, Marco P. Apolinario, Charlotte Frenkel

Abstract: Continual learning (CL) enables neural networks to adapt to non-stationary data streams by mitigating catastrophic forgetting, offering a path to robust, private, and adaptable edge devices. Yet, porting CL to real-world edge devices remains challenging for two reasons: (i) online operation, where sensor data arrives one sample at a time and the device must adapt on-the-fly, and (ii) the low footprint of edge devices, which makes traditional error backpropagation training prohibitive. Inspired by the brain's wake and sleep cycles, the former for rapid adaptation with minimal resources and the latter for long-term consolidation, we introduce Hypnos, an open-source dual-phase platform for efficient CL at the edge. In the wake phase (e.g., daily operation), Hypnos relies on a low-footprint system-on-chip with a RISC-V microcontroller unit (MCU) targeting efficient inference and supporting lightweight gradient-free prototype updates. In the sleep phase (e.g., night-time charging), where wall-plug energy and larger memory budgets are available, a smart charger performs rehearsal-based gradient updates for consolidation. Deployed on a ZCU104 field-programmable gate array (FPGA) and synthesized in a 28-nm process, Hypnos is benchmarked on the CORe50 NICv2-391 protocol. It sets a new state-of-the-art accuracy of 78.6%-81.6% for edge CL systems, with an on-device latency of 7.79s-10.54s per sample depending on activation precision, and requires $3.8\times$ fewer learning steps and $22\times$ less learning compute than quantized latent replay at higher accuracy and comparable latency.

replace-cross MADJEPA: Enhanced Multi-Stage Joint-Embedding Predictive Architecture for Medical Anomaly Detection

Authors: Ioana-Sabina Stoian, Florinel-Alin Croitoru, Vlad Hondru, Andreea Iuliana Ionescu, Andrei Paun, Radu Tudor Ionescu

Abstract: Anomaly detection in medical images is a complex task, requiring the development of a robust model using only healthy patient data. Without access to labeled data, modern approaches typically employ self-supervised learning to obtain a model of normality. Since world models promise to build strong internal representations of their environment, we consider them as suitable candidates for the medical anomaly detection environment, namely medical investigations of healthy patients. To this end, we propose MADJEPA, a medical anomaly detector based on an enhanced multi-stage joint-embedding predictive architecture (JEPA). We design a multi-stage training pipeline to produce a robust detector: (1) image-based JEPA (I-JEPA) training aims to learn a strong representation of normal training data; (2) target encoder distillation aims to modulate over-generalization of I-JEPA representations to abnormal data; (3) pseudo-anomaly classification aims to calibrate anomaly predictions by learning a tight boundary around the normal data manifold. To demonstrate our design, we carry out comprehensive experiments on six datasets covering various medical image domains (e.g. MRI, OCT, X-ray, histopathology), comparing our MADJEPA with numerous state-of-the-art methods. The results show that our carefully designed world model, MADJEPA, attains competitive results, ranking first on three datasets. Our ablation studies further confirm the utility of each training stage, indicating that I-JEPA alone is not sufficient to obtain competitive results in medical anomaly detection. We publicly release our code at https://github.com/Sabina085/madjepa.

URLs: https://github.com/Sabina085/madjepa.

replace-cross Bradley-Terry Training Can Distort Outcome Probabilities of Initially Accurate Language World Models

Authors: Xinze Shi, Litian Zhang, Zhenjie Su, Zelong Yu, Yuting Zhang, Chaozhuo Li, Qihua Feng, Zifeng Kang, Zhengxuan Hu

Abstract: Comparative feedback, in which a judge says which of two candidate outcomes is closer to what happened, is a practical way to train language world models that assign probabilities to an action's outcomes. We show that, fitted with a Bradley-Terry objective, it need not teach the true outcome probabilities and can distort accurate ones. On a simulated robot push with enumerated outcome distributions, this objective takes a model that is accurate on unseen layouts (hazard error 2.4 percentage points, pp) and moves 33 to 43 pp of its probability onto outcomes that none of a condition's enumerated branches produces. The cause is the objective, not the comparisons: its optimum, computed before training, predicts where the model ends up, and a likelihood that knows the judge's rule learns from the same comparisons without the shift. The shift is robust: it recurs on unseen conditions, under five candidate laws and a three-fold denser enumeration, and about 29 pp remain without the physically impossible events. Its cost is conditional: with those events it inflates hazard probabilities, and a threshold inherited from the pre-feedback model costs about 3 pp of utility per decision on trained and 1 to 2 pp on unseen conditions, through false rejects; without them the utility loss averages below 1 pp over six seeds, though a small regret in action choice remains. Audits of readout, response and decision trace the effect from probabilities to actions.

replace-cross VMSA: Video Memory Skill Agent for Long Video Understanding

Authors: Changin Choi, Jungwon Park, Jungmin Ko, Jimyeong Kim, Wonjong Rhee

Abstract: Long-video question answering requires retrieving and integrating relevant evidence from videos spanning hours or even days. Recent agentic approaches organize video information into external memories and iteratively retrieve evidence to answer questions. However, effective retrieval and reasoning remain challenging, as agents must determine what information to search for, which memory sources to access, and how to connect evidence across distant temporal intervals. To address these challenges, we propose Video Memory Skill Agent (VMSA), a framework that jointly designs structured video memories and memory skills for long-video understanding. VMSA constructs three complementary memory components. Segment Caption Memory and Entity Event Memory serve as searchable evidence sources, while Entity-Aware Episode Memory provides broad yet detailed video context to guide evidence retrieval and reasoning. To further support effective memory utilization, VMSA induces memory skills from successful agent trajectories, incrementally refining or extending a compact skill library to guide iterative evidence retrieval, reasoning, and verification. Across seven long-video question-answering benchmarks, VMSA achieves the highest accuracy on all three week-long egocentric benchmarks, outperforming the strongest baseline by up to 9.4% on Ego-R1 Bench, and the best or competitive performance on four additional benchmarks. Ablation studies further demonstrate the contributions of all three memory components and memory skills, with memory skills also improving temporal evidence localization.

replace-cross Self-Supervised Continuous-Time Depth Learning and Ego-Motion Estimation from Asynchronous Events

Authors: Sadiq O. Macaulay, Dipon Kumar Ghosh, Hadfield Simon

Abstract: Event cameras register brightness changes pixel by pixel with microsecond precision, whereas the dense depth that could supervise them is costly to acquire and exists only at isolated instants. Condensing the stream into frames eases learning, but at the expense of the very timing that sets event sensing apart. We propose a self-supervised approach in which each event is assigned its own depth in continuous time, and the camera motion is recovered from the same observations. Supervision arises from geometry: lifted into 3D by its predicted depth and displaced by the camera motion at the instant it fired, an event should coincide with others produced by the same edge, so any misalignment exposes an error in depth or motion. Differentiable rendering then turns these per-event estimates into dense depth maps at target times within each window, faithful to the events behind them. On MVSEC, the approach outperforms prior event-only self-supervised methods in most sequences and depth ranges, with the widest margins at night, and recovers camera rotation more accurately than an earlier unsupervised event-based method. Ablations isolate the roles of local spatiotemporal context, the temporal backbone, and the alignment objective. These results position motion-compensated event alignment as a principled source of supervision for continuous-time depth and ego-motion estimation.

replace-cross Social sycophancy differs across access modes of large language models

Authors: Alek Klem, Uwe Peters

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) can display sycophancy, a tendency to excessively praise, agree with, or validate a user. Existing studies have tested LLMs for sycophancy primarily through application programming interfaces (APIs) rather than the consumer user interfaces (UIs) through which the public commonly interacts with LLMs. Access mode (API vs. UI) may affect observed sycophancy rates. However, it remains unknown to what extent prominent models show API-UI differences in social sycophancy, which includes behaviors potentially more likely in UIs (e.g., softening warranted criticism). We tested ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini, and DeepSeek through both APIs and UIs on four dimensions of social sycophancy. Across models, UI access was generally associated with greater social sycophancy, with odds ratios up to 7.56 (95% CI [6.22, 9.20]). However, UI-API differences varied significantly across models for all sycophancy dimensions, including a pronounced reversal for DeepSeek moral sycophancy (43% UI vs. 82.3% API). Overall, our results suggest that API-based LLM evaluations may underestimate the prevalence of sycophancy in corresponding consumer-facing systems, making access conditions consequential for the external validity of LLM evaluation. To facilitate research on access-mode effects, we release 57,000 model responses and SycoUI, a browser-automation pipeline for standardized cross-interface LLM evaluation.

replace-cross Grammar-as-a-Drafter: More Tokens per Speculative Decoding Step on Grammar-Constrained Structured Outputs

Authors: John Zhang, Silky Arora

Abstract: Speculative decoding speeds up generation by letting a lightweight drafter propose tokens that the target model verifies in one forward pass. Under structured outputs (JSON schemas, regular expressions, tool-call formats), the drafter works against a constraint it does not know, so its proposals for keys, delimiters, and indentation are often rejected or invalid, even though the grammar engine already knows the next tokens. We present Grammar-as-a-Drafter (GaaD), which makes the grammar a second drafter. Before each verify step, the scheduler merges the drafter's proposal with grammar-forced tokens (out-degree 1 in the grammar automaton) into one draft row of width $K$ (which can exceed the drafter's proposal length $K_M$): matching tokens are promoted to GRAMMAR, the proposal is truncated where it disagrees, and grammar tokens fill the remaining slots. The verify step accepts GRAMMAR drafts without computing their logits, composing with any drafter without adding kernels or compilation on the critical path. Because grammars fix text rather than tokens, accepting canonical GRAMMAR drafts with probability 1 preserves grammar validity but can shift the output distribution relative to grammar-masked decoding. We implement GaaD in vllm-torchtpu and an independent Rust serving runtime, and evaluate it on TPU v7x with MTP, DFlash, and n-gram drafters on a JSON character-profile benchmark. GaaD raises average acceptance length from 2.06 to 5.43 for MTP (Qwen3.5-0.8B; 1.99 to 5.31 on Qwen3.5-35B-A3B), 1.57 to 4.13 for DFlash, and 1.30 to 3.64 for n-gram (Qwen3-4B), decoding 2.66-2.81x faster at batch size 1 and 1.14-1.70x faster at batch size 64. In the Rust runtime, MTP with GaaD is 2.23x faster on Qwen3.5-0.8B and 2.02x on Qwen3.5-397B-A17B-FP8 (1.22-1.69x at high concurrency). Under mixed traffic, GaaD finishes workloads 1.03-1.23x sooner while modestly raising plain-text TPOT.

replace-cross Seeing vs. Knowing: Counterfactual Supervision and Correction for 3D-Aware Object Editing

Authors: Jian Zahng, Zhijun Zhang

Abstract: Rotating, translating, or rescaling an object in a photograph requires revealing unseen surfaces, preserving visible appearance, and updating shadows and reflections. Reconstructed 3D assets, rendered at the target pose as a 3D state, provide explicit geometric guidance, but their imperfect textures may conflict with the photograph, while standard paired training does not explicitly teach the editor which source to trust. To address this, we propose counterfactual state supervision, which constructs controlled photo--state conflicts through hidden-surface repainting, hidden geometry with re-rendered shadows and reflections, and visible-surface texture replacement. This teaches the editor to follow the 3D state for unseen content while preserving photograph-visible appearance. However, learning this distinction does not ensure that it is maintained during sampling. We therefore introduce symmetric counterfactual correction, which uses state-side and photo-side twins, which share the photograph and the state respectively, to construct directed latent perturbations toward condition-inconsistent targets and train recovery toward the target specified by the current condition. Both strategies operate only during training, requiring no architectural changes or additional inference steps. Experiments on OBJect and SpatialEdit-Bench demonstrate strong spatial editing performance and content preservation, while controlled ablations show improved source selection and sustained responsiveness to conditioning during sampling.

replace-cross PixelOne: From Options to Pixels in a Single Forward for GUI Grounding

Authors: Kanghui Tian, Shuai Dong, Yuhan Zhu, Congcong Wang, Ji Zhao, Tianxiang Jiang, Zhixiong Zhang, Shengyuan Ding, Yi Wang, Jiaqi Wang, Yu Qiao

Abstract: GUI grounding requires precise clicks at low latency. Recent models such as GUI-Decisions and GUI-Swift predict coordinates in one forward pass using fixed global bins with local refinement. However, screenshot downsampling removes detail needed to locate small controls, while fixed bins cover larger pixel ranges at higher resolutions. We introduce PixelOne, which transfers spatial knowledge from a search teacher to a student that predicts clicks directly from screenshots at native resolution. The teacher uses VideoJEV's text-option scorer to select rows and columns and progressively zoom into candidate regions. The student inherits the teacher's parameters and matches its distributions over explored regions through spatial distillation alongside box supervision. At inference, the same decision head selects a visual token, and a lightweight pixel head predicts the click within it, all in one forward pass. A click-aligned loss encourages pixels inside the target box to score above those outside. Across four benchmarks, PixelOne-Pro-35B-A3B achieves 83.8% mean accuracy, the highest among the evaluated single-forward grounders, with a mean latency of 0.43 s per target.

replace-cross De Novo Generation of Odorant Molecules with Targeted Olfactory Receptor Activation Patterns

Authors: Kexin Zhang, Manhao Guan

Abstract: Odor perception arises from a combinatorial code in which each molecule activates a specific subset of olfactory receptors (ORs), and the resulting activation pattern determines the perceived smell. We present a framework for de novo generation of molecules predicted to match target multi-receptor activation profiles. We first construct a panoramic binding matrix over 20 ORs, then cluster it to discover representative activation patterns, and finally formulate a structured selectivity loss, combining pairwise ranking, distribution matching, and complexity constraints, to steer a molecular generator via reinforcement learning. Across nine target profiles of varying complexity, our method achieves high predicted selectivity within 18000 oracle calls and provides the largest gains on profiles that are rare under the prior, especially broad-spectrum and sparse-activator targets.

replace-cross RoboInF: A Benchmark for Instruction Fidelity in Robotic Manipulation

Authors: Jiawei Guo, Yu Huang, Dijiang Dong, Yue Liu, Peiran Ren, Wei Shen

Abstract: Precise language control requires embodied models to follow instructions governing both task outcomes and execution. Although recent methods train policies to follow increasingly detailed commands, existing benchmarks often assess instruction following alongside perception, planning, and generalization to unfamiliar scenes. Higher task success therefore offers limited evidence of closer adherence to instructions. We introduce RoboInF, a benchmark measuring how reliably manipulation policies adapt their behavior to changes in an instruction. For each of eight tasks, six to twelve expert-validated instruction branches vary the requested goal, procedure, or manipulation detail while holding the initial scene and robot state fixed. Task-specific checkers verify the terminal goal and use recorded trajectories, contacts, and event sequences to assess compliance throughout execution. The accompanying RoboInF-4K dataset pairs 4,242 expert demonstrations with simulator-derived annotations of motion and contact. We evaluate leading manipulation policies ($\pi_{0.5}$ and OpenWAM) and inference-only agents (GPT-6 Astra and Qwen3.8-Flash). All four models exhibit a gap between task completion and faithful execution, indicating substantial room for fine-grained instruction following.

replace-cross Reinforcing Diffusion Models by Instantaneous Trajectory Distribution Matching

Authors: Yihong Luo

Abstract: Forward diffusion reinforcement learning (RL)---enabling deterministic ODE sampling without trajectory backpropagation---has grown increasingly popular for efficiently aligning diffusion models. However, we reveal that existing methods essentially rely on signed reward-weighted regression (RWR), regressing the trainable policy velocity onto rollouts from a behavior distribution. Unbiased baseline subtraction implicitly assumes that the policy velocity equals the behavior marginal velocity---an idealized assumption violated under classifier-free guidance (CFG) rollouts, offline/off-policy updates, and imperfect online self-consistency. This behavior--policy mismatch breaks baseline invariance and injects baseline-induced gradient drift, destabilizing training. To resolve this issue, we propose Instantaneous Trajectory Distribution Matching (iTDM), taking the infinitesimal limit of Trajectory Distribution Matching toward a time-local reward-guided target via the posterior-score identity to yield a sample-level gradient anchored at the behavior velocity without learning an additional reward-tilted target score. Tracking this velocity with an online auxiliary denoiser guarantees exact baseline invariance at the auxiliary optimum, effectively suppressing baseline drift across both online and offline RL. Extensive experiments across compositional generation, text rendering, and preference alignment show that iTDM achieves state-of-the-art in-domain and out-of-domain performance across group-wise online/offline RL and pairwise offline RL. Notably, on the challenging GenEval2 benchmark, iTDM converges nearly 3\times faster than competing methods while scaling smoothly to 0.87 without collapse. Furthermore, iTDM scales effectively to the 5.6B FLUX.3 model, achieving better performance across both in-domain and unseen metrics. Code is available at https://github.com/Luo-Yihong/iTDM

URLs: https://github.com/Luo-Yihong/iTDM
